#3081. Mortgage Rates Today, Tuesday, November 3: Still Moving Lower

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased again today, giving buyers modest relief from rates above 7%.

Mortgage rates are continuing to cool off as we head into November.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 6.93% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and 17 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

A significant drop is eye-catching, but bear in mind that you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. Since September, mortgage rates have been hovering around 7%. That’s a full percentage point higher than they were at the beginning of 2026.

This week’s employment data could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other — more on that below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.43% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3079. Five Connectivity Requirements For The Intelligent Edge

Michael De Nil is the CEO and cofounder at Morse Micro—reinventing Wi-Fi for IoT.

​The first wave of connected infrastructure was built around a simple assumption: Devices would collect data, networks would transport it and most of the intelligence would live somewhere else, normally in the cloud.

Edge AI changes that architecture. Cameras, building systems, industrial equipment and energy assets are increasingly becoming intelligent endpoints capable of analyzing data and responding locally. As that happens, the network is no longer simply a transport layer. It becomes part of the system that allows distributed intelligence to function.

That means organizations need to evaluate connectivity differently. The question is no longer simply, “How fast is the network?” The more useful question is whether the network can support intelligence reliably across the environments where it will actually operate. That comes down to five requirements.

Intelligent infrastructure is often distributed well beyond traditional indoor coverage. Cameras may sit at property edges. Smart meters and home batteries may be installed in garages or utility rooms. Industrial equipment may operate across large facilities. Energy devices may be located outdoors or behind multiple physical barriers.

In those environments, adding more access points, gateways or cabling can significantly increase cost and complexity. The network therefore has to be designed around where devices actually need to operate, not around where connectivity happens to be easiest to deploy.

Edge AI devices already have to budget power for their sensing activities and the local processing. If connectivity consumes too much of that budget, the overall system becomes more difficult to deploy and maintain.

This is especially important for battery-powered cameras, sensors, meters and other devices expected to operate for extended periods without frequent maintenance. The right question is not whether a wireless technology can deliver maximum throughput; it is whether it can deliver the performance the application needs without overwhelming the device’s energy budget. That makes power efficiency part of the architecture, not simply a component specification.

At the other end of the spectrum, many low-power networks were designed around simple telemetry: a meter reading, sensor value or periodic status message.

The intelligent edge increasingly moves richer information. A camera may transmit images, event metadata or diagnostics. Industrial equipment may send more detailed operating data. Devices may require software or model updates. Energy systems may need to coordinate multiple distributed assets.

That creates a middle ground between high-throughput conventional networking and narrowband sensor connectivity. Organizations may not need maximum bandwidth everywhere, but they increasingly need meaningful bandwidth, enough to support richer data and evolving workloads without abandoning power efficiency or range.

As more intelligence moves into physical infrastructure, the consequences of a compromised endpoint increase. A simple sensor reporting temperature is one thing. A network of cameras, access systems, industrial equipment or energy assets making local decisions is another. Strong authentication, encryption, secure update mechanisms and established network-management practices therefore need to be considered from the beginning.

The same applies to network complexity. Adding proprietary gateways or isolated networking layers may solve a short-term coverage problem but can create additional systems for IT and operations teams to secure, integrate and maintain. The simpler the architecture, the easier it becomes to manage security consistently.

The intelligent edge will not be built around one device type, one vendor or one networking technology. Buildings may combine security cameras, HVAC systems, occupancy sensors and energy equipment. Industrial environments may bring together machines, sensors and monitoring platforms. Utilities may need to connect meters, batteries, solar systems and EV infrastructure. Those systems need to work within broader IP-based architectures rather than becoming isolated connectivity islands.

Standards-based networking becomes increasingly important because it allows intelligent endpoints to integrate more naturally into existing infrastructure and management systems. Newer approaches such as Wi-Fi HaLow, based on the IEEE 802.11ah standard, illustrate how the Wi-Fi ecosystem itself is evolving toward this model by combining sub-GHz reach, lower power operation, IP networking and sufficient bandwidth for richer edge applications.

The broader point is not that one wireless technology will replace every other option. Traditional Wi-Fi will remain critical for high-throughput local networks. Cellular will continue to serve mobility and wide-area applications. Other low-power technologies will remain useful for simple sensing.​

The shift is architectural. Organizations building Edge AI systems should stop evaluating connectivity around a single headline specification and instead design around the workload. Where will the device operate? How much power can it consume? What information must it exchange? How will it be secured? And how will thousands of endpoints fit into a larger network?

The intelligent edge is changing how computing is distributed across the physical world. Connectivity needs to evolve with it. For leaders designing the next generation of intelligent infrastructure, the network should no longer be treated as an afterthought. It should be designed as part of the intelligence itself.

#3075. Apple Stock Is Up 22%, but 1 Slowing Business Could Test That Valuation

Apple stock has outperformed the market this year, but the growth of its services business is slowing.

Apple (AAPL +5.31%) stock is up about 22% this year through Nov. 3, roughly double the S&P 500's (^GSPC +1.58%) 11% gain. That run has pushed the stock to a valuation of about 38 times earnings. One reason Apple has deserved that premium is steady growth in its high-margin services business, making the recent slowdown worth a closer look.

In Apple's fiscal 2026 fourth quarter, which ended Sept. 26, services growth slowed to 10% year over year, down from about 12% the prior quarter, and management said it expects 8% to 9% in fiscal 2027 Q1, citing headwinds from third-party licensing and shifts in foreign currency exchange rates. Notably, in Apple's annual report for fiscal 2026, it did not describe the App Store as a driver of services segment growth, listing only advertising and cloud services.

The slowing growth of the services business matters because it is Apple's profit engine. Services revenue came with a 76% gross margin last fiscal year, nearly double its 39% gross margin on products. And services accounted for 40% of Apple's gross profit.

Earnings grew 13.5% year over year in fiscal Q4, but analysts are modeling for annual growth of less than 11% over the long term. If growth in services continues to weaken, investors may re-rate the stock to a lower earnings multiple, which could limit its upside potential.

The rest of Apple's business looks strong. Revenue rose 15% in fiscal 2026 to a record $479 billion as iPhone sales grew 17% last quarter and Mac sales jumped 24%. Apple expects the current holiday quarter to be "the best quarter for revenue in the history of the company," as it will be the first full quarter of sales for the iPhone Duo, Apple's first foldable smartphone.

With the stock trading at about 38 times earnings, investors are paying for a lot of anticipated growth. The one thing to watch is whether the next quarter's services growth lands within or above the 8% to 9% forecast range. If it falls short, the stock's current premium valuation could become harder to justify.

John Ballard has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Apple. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Both the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard®  and Citi Strata Premier® Card can help you rack up AAdvantage miles. The former earns them directly; the latter transfers points to American Airlines at a 1:1 ratio. So which one is better for American Airlines flyers?

For earning rewards, the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the winner. Although it's not an American Airlines credit card, it can make it easier to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases with its higher earning rates and the option to transfer ThankYou points to American.

Still, the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® can help you achieve AAdvantage elite status and offers perks when flying American Airlines, including a free checked bag and preferred boarding. In 2027, the annual fee will increase, but the card will add new bonus categories and other benefits.

The Citi Strata Premier® Card is a better fit for travelers who want to earn American Airlines miles without committing all their rewards to a single airline program.

Although the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® is an American Airlines credit card, the Citi Strata Premier® Card can be a faster way to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases.

That's because Citi Strata Premier® Card holders can transfer ThankYou points to American Airlines AAdvantage — and several other loyalty programs — at a 1:1 ratio.

Currently, the Citi Strata Premier® Card offers more rewards than the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® in every bonus category. On Jan. 24, 2027, when the benefits on the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® change, that will change — but only in a couple of narrowly defined categories: select streaming services and eligible Rides and Rails purchases, which include taxis, rideshares, subways and trains. #3072. Financial Language AI In The Cloud: Matching The Model To The Job

Srinivas Chippagiri is a technology leader with over 15 years experience in cloud computing and distributed systems across multiple domains.

Financial firms process enormous volumes of text every day, from news wires and regulatory filings to earnings commentary and social media posts. Large language models (LLMs), the systems behind modern chatbots, offer new ways to analyze this information at scale. But putting these systems to work raises questions that go beyond model performance: Where will the model run, how much will each request cost and who is accountable for its output?​​

In my experience building enterprise AI and cloud systems, teams that settle those questions early tend to ship more dependable systems than teams that start with the model alone.

​Sentiment analysis labels a passage as positive, negative or neutral. It compresses a long document into something a dashboard or an analyst’s queue can use. It is also a narrow signal. A sentence can be upbeat in tone and still say little about a company’s prospects. Reading sentiment is not forecasting markets, and a sentiment label is not a prediction.​

Bigger is not automatically better. Models adapted to financial text, such as FinBERT, a language model pretrained for financial sentiment analysis, illustrate how domain adaptation can make a compact system competitive on focused reading tasks, even with limited labeled data.​

​That matters most at cloud scale. Cloud computing, as NIST defines it, offers “on-demand network access to a shared pool of configurable computing resources” that can expand and contract quickly. Inference (running the model on new text) may be billed per request or token through managed services, or per instance-hour when self-hosted. Either way, a compact model that performs comparably can meaningfully reduce the bill across millions of daily headlines. I’ve seen organizations find that a smaller model works best for handling routine sentiment analysis while reserving a larger model for more complex passages where the meaning is less clear or depends on additional context.​

Fine-tuning, which means continuing a model’s training on specialized text, makes this practical. Efficient techniques such as QLoRA compress the base model and train only a small set of added parameters, lowering the hardware needed to adapt large models and bringing experimentation within reach of smaller teams.​

Neutral and hedged language is often the hardest case. Financial writing often uses cautious language to describe results that meet expectations or outlooks that remain uncertain. These statements can be difficult to classify because they convey neither clearly positive nor clearly negative sentiment. ​

A single headline accuracy score can conceal this. A model may look strong overall while quietly mishandling the ambiguous middle, which is where analysts most need care. Teams that examine errors across each sentiment category and test models on real-world financial text, including ambiguous or inconsistently worded passages, can identify weaknesses that overall accuracy scores and clean benchmark datasets may overlook. This helps them catch classification errors before those errors carry through to downstream reports.​

A live feed of news and filings arrives in bursts around earnings season and market events. Autoscaling, which adds or removes servers as traffic changes, can absorb those bursts, but only once a latency budget (the longest acceptable wait for a result) is defined. Without one, scaling decisions become guesswork and costs drift upward.​

Data location matters, too. Managed AI services reduce operational work, while self-hosting open models inside a private cloud network may suit organizations handling regulated or confidential data, or those with data-residency obligations. The trade-off is between convenience and control.​

Cloud-native tooling can support governance. Audit logs record who ran what, and access controls limit who can change a model. Monitoring can flag drift, meaning a gradual mismatch between the text a model was tuned on and the text it now sees. Human review queues can route low-confidence or neutral-looking cases to an analyst. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework, a voluntary guide for identifying and managing AI risks, offers a useful anchor for organizing these practices.​

Organizations that build oversight into the architecture from the start, rather than adding it after launch, tend to find audits and incident reviews far less painful.​​

I expect AI for financial text to become less about running the largest model and more about matching a model to each job. Compact, domain-tuned systems may handle high-volume reading while larger ones may take on difficult reasoning, and people will supervise both. Evaluation and monitoring tooling will probably matter as much as the models themselves.​

When it comes to AI for financial text, the best infrastructure choice is often the model that is accurate enough, affordable at scale and explainable enough to oversee, rather than the largest one available.​​​

#3070. I Love Brookfield Renewable for 1 Reason, and It’s Not the Dividend

The income draws investors in, but the growth story is what makes Brookfield Renewable so attractive.

Plenty of investors own Brookfield Renewable (BEPC +2.63%)(BEP +2.01%) for the dividend. With a 5% yield and 17 years of delivering at least 5% annual dividend growth, it's a high-quality income stock.

However, Brookfield's dividend isn't the main thing I like about the renewable energy stock. What I love about Brookfield is its high-powered growth rate, which significantly enhances its total return potential.

Brookfield Renewable recently reported its third-quarter results. The clean energy company generated $0.51 per unit of funds from operations (FFO), up 11% year over year. Meanwhile, it has grown its FFO per unit by 12% over the last 12 months. That's robust growth for a company with a 5% dividend yield.

The company has multiple growth drivers, including inflation-linked rate increases, rising margins, development projects, and its value-enhancing capital recycling strategy. Brookfield has invested about $700 million in two new businesses over the past few months (Boralex and Aypa), funded by $850 million in asset sales. Meanwhile, it has delivered 5.6 gigawatts (GW) of new renewable energy capacity so far this year. These investments should support its continued growth.

Brookfield expects to deploy more than $11 billion in capital over the next five years. It's ramping up its renewable energy development capabilities, with 10 GW expected to be delivered next year and 11 GW in 2028, while continuing to pursue M&A transactions. These and other catalysts drive its expectation of delivering more than 10% annual FFO per share growth going forward. Add that to its 5%-yielding dividend, and Brookfield's total returns could approach 15% annually.

While Brookfield's high-yielding dividend is nice, its strong growth rate is what positions it to deliver high-powered total returns in the coming years.

Matt DiLallo has positions in Brookfield Renewable and Brookfield Renewable Partners. The Motley Fool recommends Brookfield Renewable and Brookfield Renewable Partners. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3069. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#3066. Bill Ackman Says Long-Term Investors Can Benefit From a Broken Market. Here's the Price I'd Pay for Netflix Stock.

His fund reported a stake in the streamer in August, and its shares now trade at around half their peak. Is that cheap enough?

Bill Ackman isn't impressed by how the stock market has been acting recently. On Tuesday, Nov. 3, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management told CNBC's "Squawk Box" that markets seem broken to him sometimes, with stocks trading at prices he can't understand. But he also saw an upside for one type of investor.

Long-term investors can benefit when they get to buy shares at prices that are way too low, Ackman said.

According to CNBC, the stocks he's especially bullish on include Netflix (NFLX +4.68%), where he reported a stake in August.

And Netflix has definitely gotten cheaper. Shares are near $66 as of this writing, about half the $133.91 record close they set in June 2025 (split-adjusted, after last November's 10-for-1 stock split).

Has Netflix stock dropped that far, then?

I don't think it's there yet. But I think it's getting close.

Netflix's third-quarter earnings per share climbed 49% from a year ago, to $0.87, and operating income grew 37% to around $4.5 billion, for an operating margin of 34.6%. Granted, both numbers got help from an easy comparison. In the year-ago quarter, Netflix booked an expense of around $619 million linked to a dispute with Brazilian tax authorities, which the company said cut its operating margin that quarter by over 5 percentage points.

Yet the business grew its profits without that help. Add the Brazil charge back to the third quarter of 2025, and Netflix's operating income climbed around 15% year over year, with its operating margin about a point higher than the year before.

Buybacks are helping, too. Netflix spent $4.8 billion repurchasing 63.9 million shares in the third quarter, its biggest quarter of buybacks ever. And its diluted share count has dropped around 3% year over year.

Sales are the weak spot. Revenue growth reached 17.6% in the fourth quarter of 2025 and has slowed every quarter since, to 16.2%, 13.4%, and then 11.8% last quarter.

Management's guidance is for around 11.5% growth in the fourth quarter. Netflix also cut its 2026 ad revenue forecast to around $2.7 billion from about $3 billion, citing a slower-than-expected rollout of some ad features.

In the first quarter, Netflix got a $2.8 billion termination fee linked to its Warner Bros. acquisition that fell through. Its first-quarter earnings per share landed at $1.23, far above the company's own pre-quarter forecast of $0.76.

Through September, Netflix earned $2.90 per share, and its fourth-quarter guidance is for another $0.67. That's around $3.57 for the year. Plug in the $0.76 forecast for the first quarter's reported figure, though, and 2026 comes to around $3.10 -- a rough stand-in for what the business earned without the fee.

On average, analysts expect Netflix to earn around $3.77 a share in 2027, about 22% above this year's number without the fee.

I think the estimate is reachable if margins keep widening. But it might get tougher every quarter that revenue growth slows, because more of the earnings growth then has to come from margin gains and buybacks.

At around $66, Netflix stock costs about 18 times earnings on analysts' average estimate for 2027. For a company still widening its operating margin, the stock arguably looks reasonable. But reasonable isn't the same as a bargain.

I'd pay about 16 times the 2027 earnings estimate, or around $60 a share.

The reason is room for error. If Netflix's 2027 earnings fell 10% below the estimate, a $60 stock would still cost about 18 times earnings -- the same price-to-earnings ratio investors pay now for the full estimate.

After all, a 10% miss isn't hard to imagine for a company whose sales growth has slowed for three straight quarters.

This is a slightly tougher bar than I used last week. On Oct. 28, I put Netflix on a list of stocks worth owning through 2030, and on Oct. 30, I said I'd rather own Apple. Neither view has changed. But owning a company through 2030 and picking a price for new shares today are two different calls.

Netflix's profits keep climbing even as its stock price has been cut in half. At about $60, or around 10% under where shares trade as I write, I'd be glad to open a position and add to it slowly. At $66, the stock isn't quite there.

Daniel Sparks and his clients have positions in Apple. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Apple and Netflix. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3064. Mortgage Rates Today, Wednesday, November 4: Back Up

Mortgage rates jumped after stronger-than-expected jobs data raised expectations for a Fed rate hike next month.

Mortgage rates are higher today on the heels of strong labor market data released yesterday.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.07% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points higher than yesterday but 25 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

Mortgage rates are always on the move, so if you're tracking rates day-to-day, you're going to see a lot of volatility.

As for today’s jump: Job openings hit a four-month high of 7.5 million in September, according to the Labor Department’s Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey released yesterday. Hiring picked up, too, while layoffs declined. Friday’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other.

To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.57% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3062. SLR Investment (SLRC) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Management flags rising defaults even as record originations keep coming.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

SLR Investment Corp. (SLRC +3.31%) reported third quarter results characterized by record originations and stable net investment income that fully supported its dividend. Management reported a strategic focus on specialty finance, with 95% of the $575.1 million in new investments allocated to collateral-based lending rather than traditional cash flow loans. The company maintained a conservative leverage profile at 1.1x net debt-to-equity and reported significant liquidity with over $850 million in available capital. While net asset value per share declined slightly to $17.83 due to the performance of a single nonaccrual loan, the company noted that its broader portfolio remains healthy with limited exposure to cyclical 2021 and 2022 vintages. Additionally, the firm utilized its stock repurchase program to acquire $10 million in shares post-quarter end, which is expected to be accretive to future net asset value per share.

AI and the new economy build-out dramatically impact capital flows, commerce, and ultimately risk. As a result, questions about where we are on the credit cycle have grown louder throughout the year. Expectations for interest coverage and direct lending facilities are moderating. We first expressed a more cautious view about the potential for rising private credit losses late last year. That view rested on our 20-plus history of managing private credit vehicles and recognizing that the industry was approaching the middle stages of a credit cycle.

We believe these signs can no longer be dismissed. They are clearly visible through the lens of rising defaults and realized losses. The simple average net asset value per share has declined more than 5% for public BDCs year to date and Fitch ratings recently indicated that the trailing 12-month default rate is the highest on record as of the end of the third quarter. Against this backdrop, we continue to believe investors are looking for private credit portfolios that are less exposed to areas of private credit exuberance during the past 5 years. We believe our comprehensive portfolio of largely commercial finance asset-positioned assets positions us to provide durable current income and total return.

We think the strength of our portfolio today reflects how we structure and select investments not where we sit in the current cycle or the amount of capital we need to deploy. Many of today's market participants have never underwritten through a true downturn, as rapid capital formation loosened cash flow underwriting as non-listed BDCs grew into tens of billions of dollars under management in a very short period. The stress was concentrated in the 2021 and 2022 vintages underwritten at high leverage in the low interest rate environment and are now facing a large wave of cyclicals and software loan maturities. Importantly, less than 15% of our comprehensive portfolio is invested in these vintages.

Floating rate direct lending would normally benefit from an increase in base rates. But higher financing costs, margin compression and rising credit losses are thus far offsetting this tailwind. Continued lackluster M&A activity and excess supply of capital further won the supply-demand imbalance and extend the highly competitive environment for good cash flow assets. As a result, sourcing good risk return and sponsor finance remains challenging. We expect defaults and liability management exercises to rise throughout the next year. This is a typical credit cycle downturn. Not a crisis, a la 2008. However, we believe that defaults will be higher than many expect.

Weaker documentation and unitranche structures that sit deeper in the capital structure leave less cushion beneath lenders, so loss given default rates could run higher than many assume. The current environment favors experienced collateral based lenders as sponsors of capital structures that cannot refinance in the traditional market need liquidity and working capital solutions. Our portfolio is built for this environment. It primarily consists of first lien loans against collateral that we can monitor and liquidate selective cash flow exposure and a small watch list. Our ABL teams are a countercyclical sourcing engine when liquidity and capital are scarce. In this market phase, credit dispersion is expected to widen and manager and asset selection will matter more.

We will stay disciplined and are prepared for either a typical downturn or a more benign outcome. Nearly all of our originations in the third quarter were in specialty finance. And we expect that focus to continue for the foreseeable future. Turning to our Q3 results. #3060. ​The AI Advantage Hiding In The Foundation Of Your Drupal CMS

Jon Stewart is the President of ZenSource, an open source platform combining CMS, enterprise level cloud hosting and support.

Through my work helping organizations build and manage Drupal websites, I know that Drupal has never been the loudest name in content management. While newer platforms often competed on simplicity and flash, Drupal kept investing in something less glamorous: structure, governance and clean data. Now, as AI changes how people find information, those quieter investments are paying off.

Search is shifting from ranking pages to being understood. AI assistants and answer engines don’t just index your site; they also extract, summarize and cite it. A buyer researching vendors increasingly gets an AI-generated answer before visiting a single website. Whether your organization appears in that answer depends less on keywords and more on how clearly your content can be interpreted. Platforms built on structured, well-governed content are the ones best suited to help companies succeed.

If you’ve been on Drupal for years, you’ve likely been doing AI optimization without calling it that.

Semantic URLs: Clean, human-readable paths signal topic and hierarchy to crawlers and language models, telling an AI system what a page is about before it reads a word.

Structured Content: Content types, fields and taxonomies break information into labeled pieces. An event has a date, a location and a speaker. That is exactly what retrieval systems need to answer questions accurately instead of guessing from a wall of text.

Machine-Readable Metadata: Schema.org markup, metatags and sitemaps help AI identify entities, authors and relationships.

API-First Architecture: Content flows through standard APIs to AI applications, agents and new channels without rebuilding.

Accessibility And Multilingual Support: Semantic markup built for screen readers is also easier for machines to parse, and structured translations extend your visibility across markets.

Governance: Revisions, workflows and permissions keep content accurate. When AI quotes you, accuracy is your brand.​​

​When I help long-time Drupal organizations prepare for AI-driven discovery, there are two key points I focus on: making structured content easy to maintain and introducing AI capabilities without sacrificing control. ​​

In my work, one of the most common reasons content models break down is that editors bypass structure when it slows them down. In higher education, for example, I regularly see program pages rebuilt as free-form layouts, with deadlines and tuition tucked into tables or images rather than maintained in dedicated fields. The information is available to human readers, but it becomes harder for AI systems to retrieve and interpret reliably.

This is why the authoring experience matters as much as the underlying architecture. For Drupal users, visual editing tools like Drupal Canvas can help bridge the gap between editorial flexibility and structured data, but it’s also important to design content templates that give editors flexibility while keeping key information in dedicated, reusable fields.

AI can help organizations retrieve information, answer questions and identify opportunities to improve existing content. But the ability to generate a plausible response does not establish that the response is accurate, current or appropriate to publish.​

In practice, this means grounding AI applications in trusted organizational content, making their sources visible and establishing clear approval requirements for consequential changes. It also means considering how easily an organization can change AI providers as capabilities, costs and requirements evolve. An architecture that separates content management from model selection gives technology leaders more flexibility without requiring them to surrender governance.​​

These principles can ultimately apply regardless of which content management system an organization uses, and the objective is to make reliable information easier for both people and machines to use while preserving the controls that keep it trustworthy.​​​​

Here are some additional changes that I’ve seen make the greatest difference:

• Audit your content model. Years of quick fixes often leave important information buried in body text. I regularly find event dates, locations and eligibility rules written into paragraphs, which AI assistants then misread or skip. Usually the fields to hold that information already exist and simply went unused, so the fix is migration and editorial discipline, not a rebuild. Moving key details into dedicated fields makes them visible to AI.

• Expand structured data. Make sure organizations, people, events, articles and FAQs carry consistent Schema.org markup.

• Clean up URLs and redirects. Consistent alias patterns and resolved redirect chains help crawlers and AI systems map your site.

• Retire stale content. Archive or update outdated pages so AI isn’t summarizing information you no longer stand behind.

• Add an accuracy check to governance. Most workflows ask whether content is ready to publish. For high-stakes content such as pricing, eligibility and policy, also ask whether you’d stand behind it if AI quoted it out of context.

• Stay current. Running a supported version is the gateway to the AI capabilities arriving now.

• Is content structured or trapped in page-sized rich text fields?

• Can AI tools and agents access it through open APIs and standards?

• Can you switch AI models as the market changes?

• Do humans stay in control, with review, audit trails and permissions?

• Do you own your data and hosting choices?

A “no” to any of these points is a sign that it might be worth further developing your strategy.

Many long-time Drupal organizations are approaching a major version upgrade, a consolidation or a redesign. Treat it as a content modeling exercise, not a lift-and-shift. Moving messy content to a newer version simply relocates the mess. The AI value comes from restructuring as you go: defining content types around how your audiences ask questions, establishing consistent taxonomies and setting governance rules from day one.

AI is also changing the economics of this work. It can help map legacy content to new structures, flag duplicates, suggest tags and clean up inconsistencies that once required extensive manual effort. Discovery, stakeholder review and QA still take real time, but the heavy lifting in between is faster than many organizations expect.

The organizations that win in AI-driven discovery won’t be the ones with the most AI features. They’ll be the ones whose content is easiest to understand, trust and reuse.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3059. AI Agents: How Meta’s Muse, OpenAI’s Dots Work, What They Can Do, Safety Worries

The tech industry has long been obsessed with the idea of creating a powerful digital assistant — whether it be a chatbot, a smart speaker or robot. Artificial intelligence offers the promise of realizing that goal.

#3058. LTC Properties (LTC) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

SHOP now drives 45% of NOI as the REIT recycles capital into senior housing.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

LTC Properties, Inc. (LTC +1.16%) reported a significant strategic transition as its Seniors Housing Operating Portfolio (SHOP) reached 45% of total annualized net operating income (NOI), with a target to hit 50% by year-end. Management indicated that the company is actively recycling capital from lower-growth triple-net lease assets into the higher-growth SHOP segment, which now includes 50 communities and 14 operators. The company increased its 2026 investment guidance to $1 billion following record acquisition activity and maintains a pro forma liquidity position of over $900 million. Management stated that the current strategy triples the pro forma internal growth rate of the portfolio as it moves toward a long-term goal of generating 75% of NOI from SHOP assets by 2028.

Operator: Welcome to the LTC Properties Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Call. At this time, all participants are in a listen-only mode. Joining us on today's call are Pam Kessler, Co-President and Co-Chief Executive Officer, Clint Malin, Co-President and Co-Chief Executive Officer, Cece Chikhale, Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer and Treasurer, Gibson Satterwhite, Executive Vice President of Asset Management, Dave Boitano, Executive Vice President and Chief Investment Officer. Before management begins its presentation, please know that today's comments, including the question and answer session, may include forward-looking statements subject to risks and uncertainties that may cause actual results and events to differ materially.

These risks and uncertainties are detailed in LTC Properties filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission from time to time, including the company's most recent 10-K dated December 31, 2025. LTC undertakes no obligation to revise or update these forward-looking statements to reflect events or circumstances after the date of this presentation. Please note, this event is being recorded. I would now like to turn the conference over to LTC management.

Clint Malin: Good morning, everyone, and thank you for joining us. Our SHOP strategy is delivering from a growth perspective and has fundamentally reshaped LTC's future. We launched SHOP in May of 2025 and are rapidly transforming from a lower growth triple-net lease and lending platform into a SHOP-focused REIT with a higher intrinsic growth profile. Last quarter, we increased our 2026 midpoint acquisition guidance by 50% to $900 million and have since surpassed that guidance with approximately $950 million in SHOP acquisitions closed through October. We expect total acquisitions for the year of approximately $1 billion, at which point SHOP will have grown from zero communities into a $1.6 billion portfolio over 19 months.

Additionally, we have expanded our SHOP operator relationships to 14, including one new relationship added last month. 2026 has been a historic year for LTC. As we continue to focus on the tremendous opportunities ahead of us, our sights are set firmly on 2027. To date, we have $200 million of SHOP acquisitions that are expected to close in the first quarter of next year, which will also add a new SHOP operator relationship to our portfolio. At an expected $1 billion of SHOP acquisitions in 2026, the portfolio will represent 50% of LTC's pro forma annualized NOI at year-end.

At this level, our pro forma internal growth rate triples, and as SHOP continues to expand as a larger share of our total portfolio, our expected growth rate increases even further, significantly enhancing our ability to organically grow Core FFO and FAD per share above historical levels. As we have previously discussed, at our current SHOP acquisition pace, we see a pathway to generating approximately 75% of our annualized NOI from SHOP by the end of 2028. This timeline can be accelerated should we identify additional opportunities to recycle capital out of older and lower growth triple-net leased assets. We are proud of what we have achieved, but even more excited about what lies ahead.

Transformation is no longer a future objective. It is happening now, and LTC is entering a new chapter of growth, scale, and value creation. Gibson will walk us through LTC's operating portfolio.

Gibson Satterwhite: Thank you, Clint. We are continuing to optimize risk-adjusted returns and increase the intrinsic growth profile of our own portfolio by recycling capital into SHOP. in the third quarter, we generated about $465 million of gross proceeds by selling triple-net assets at an implied cap rate on our rent of approximately 5.5%. The $180 million Prestige loan is currently slated to pay off on November 24. Going into next year, we will continue to capitalize on opportunities to recycle capital as we look to further accelerate our portfolio transformation. We are also taking action on the 10-property portfolio subject to market-based rent resets. Of these 10 properties, we recently began the sales process for three.

Another two of the 10 properties have been converted into a long-term lease with initial annualized rent of $2.4 million. The lease contains a SHOP conversion option that could be exercised as early as the first quarter of 2027. For the remaining five properties, we plan to negotiate a long-term lease in advance of the current lease expiration next year. We expect to recognize rent of $780,000 in the fourth quarter from these five properties. With respect to our core SHOP portfolio, we are narrowing our 2026 guidance to $53.5 million-$55.5 million, half a million dollars below our prior range at the new midpoint of $54.5 million.

The reduction is primarily due to a change in care and unit occupancy mix and a Medicaid rate decrease received in Q3, retroactive to July 1, which affects two stand-alone memory care communities in our portfolio. Excluding these two communities, Medicaid as a percentage of our SHOP revenue is approximately 3% and is primarily offered as a spend-down option in states with rates on par with private pay rates. For private pay, our operators have been able to implement strong rate increases, and we expect that to continue into 2027. The current guidance range for our core SHOP portfolio represents approximately 11%-15% pro forma growth for the prior year and 13% at the midpoint.

The underlying fundamentals of our business are strong. As we've outlined in prior calls, we believe that our core SHOP portfolio can achieve high single to low double-digit NOI growth with a 200 basis point spread between RevPOR and ExPOR, even at zero occupancy growth. To be clear, we do expect that our operators will continue to drive occupancy growth going forward. We're excited about the new and expanded operator relationships we've cultivated, the quality of the communities in our portfolio, and their markets. We believe that our SHOP portfolio is well-positioned to deliver the growth necessary to transform the intrinsic growth profile of our overall portfolio as we continue to increase our SHOP mix.

Now Dave will discuss our recent investment activity.

Dave Boitano: Thanks, Gibson. We are executing successfully in a dynamic SHOP acquisition market, maintaining our strong momentum and enhancing LTC's growth trajectory. LTC's differentiated approach centers on aligning quality assets with exceptional operators in markets with strong demand fundamentals, creating a portfolio built both for performance today and growth over time. We've established a strong pool of operators who are well-suited to the profile of communities we're acquiring, allowing us to move quickly as opportunities arise. SHOP has opened a much larger opportunity set for LTC, and we are seeing significantly greater deal flow in 2026 than at any time in our history. We have been selective and strategic in vetting opportunities to pursue only acquisitions that fit our buy box.

Illustrating our focus, the acquisitions we have closed in 2026 represent less than 5% of screen deal volume. Heading into 2027, we expect deal flow to remain strong, especially in a higher interest rate environment, and as loans originated at lower rates mature and drive owners to consider monetization ahead of those maturities. NIC estimates that nearly $40 billion in loans will mature over the next four years. We are building our portfolio methodically. Investment decisions are guided by key fundamentals, including occupancy, asset quality, property scale, unit mix, size, and configuration, and market dynamics to assemble a portfolio positioned to perform for the long run. LTC's collaborative, execution-oriented culture resonates with operators, sellers, and intermediaries.

Our reputation for responsiveness, transaction certainty, and disciplined execution reinforces our relationships, enhances our strategic advantage, and drives a robust acquisition pipeline. 2026 has been a year of unprecedented growth, positioning SHOP acquisitions to reach $1 billion by the end of the year. This more than doubles our prior year investment activity, making 2026 the most active year in LTC's history and setting the stage for a new LTC growth era. The opportunities we see in the market today, together with our growing operator relationships and sourcing network, give us confidence in our ability to sustain our momentum into 2027 and beyond. If you look at the other piece of that, which was slightly less than half of that change, that was really due to unit mix across a little bit more IL than AL. It's buildings that have both.

You're not reducing staffing levels for that. A little less acuity in memory care around the margins. Again, you're not reducing staffing levels for that. A little trend, quarterly trend to lower RevPOR memory care units. That we just didn't We feel like that'll sort itself out over time. We're not really concerned about that. We just didn't model that to bounce back in Q4. What we're seeing on the ground, the top line result, in that kind of RevPOR, us bringing RevPOR down, is not what we're seeing on the ground.

What we're seeing on the ground with respect to, if you just look at like-for-like care unit type, market, our operators have been, throughout the year, been able to implement strong rate increases. As we head into next year, I think we're optimistic that we can do, that they can do the same. Kind of talking about in-place mid-single digits to high single digits of in-place rate growth. We feel like the fundamentals are really strong on that. On the expenses, labor is the bulk of your cost structure. Clint Malin: Also, to add to Gibson's comment, there's also the acquisitions that we have invested in, we've retained the existing manager for the most part, other than a few opportunities. That leads to consistency of leadership, staffing, familiarity with the markets, the buildings to be able to drive RevPOR growth as well as manage expense. We're excited about that.

#3057. Pet Insurance on Your Mind? Buy It Now, Not Later

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

If your pet is young and healthy, the cost of coverage may not seem worth it. But that math can change fast.

Senior Writer & Licensed Insurance Specialist

It feels natural to protect your pet’s health with insurance in the same way you protect your loved ones with health insurance. After all, fur babies are part of the family.

But if you’ve shopped for pet insurance recently, you might have cringed at the cost. Should you really pay hundreds of dollars every year for something you might not use? For some pet parents, the answer is yes, even if their pet is young and healthy. And that’s partly because of how pet insurance handles pre-existing conditions.

A pre-existing condition is any illness or injury that shows symptoms before your pet insurance policy takes effect. Pet insurers usually won’t cover these conditions.

Dr. Rebecca Greenstein, chief veterinarian at Kleinburg Veterinary Hospital and veterinary expert for rover.com, says she’s seeing an increase in claim denials based on pre-existing conditions. “The whole point of insurance is you want broad spectrum coverage for a variety of things,” she says. “By the time they're excluding major organ systems and anything related to that, you may be paying monthly for coverage that doesn't actually help you when you need to go make a claim.”

If you’re considering pet insurance, it’s best to buy a policy before your pet needs it.

Your pet’s pre-existing conditions will determine which treatments and procedures insurers cover down the road.

Say your dog has a history of allergies. If you buy a policy after your four-footed friend has shown symptoms, pet insurers typically won’t pay for allergy shots or other treatments. The exclusion might also apply to related problems like itchiness or ear infections, says Dr. Michael Goldstein, medical director at Billerica Veterinary Hospital and Thrive Pet Healthcare.

He offers the example of a dog who goes to the vet for a hotspot one year and an ear infection the next. If you got pet insurance for your dog between those visits, the insurer might deny your claim because both issues could be caused by underlying allergies.

The best way around this type of scenario is to buy coverage when your pet is young, before any symptoms develop.

» MORE: The best pet insurance for pre-existing conditions

It’s tricky to figure out if pet insurance will actually save you money in the long run. The most common type of plan covers accidents and illness. It costs an average of $836 for dogs and $435 for cats each year, according to the North American Pet Health Insurance Association (NAPHIA). You could pay these premiums for years before an emergency surgery or chronic illness makes its way to your financial doorstep.

It’s impossible to know for sure whether your pet will stay healthy or run up sky-high vet bills. But you can assess the potential value of pet insurance by looking at common chronic conditions that pets develop with age and how much it costs to treat them.

For instance, “cats generally, as younger animals, don't have a lot of problems,” Goldstein says. “But as they get older, there are definitely common issues. Older cats in particular get chronic kidney disease.”

In cats over age 10, the Cornell Feline Health Center estimates that as many as 40% have chronic kidney disease. The annual cost to treat this condition can top $1,200.

Another example is atopic dermatitis from skin allergies, a common ailment affecting as many as 15% of dogs, according to the Cornell Richard P. Riney Canine Health Center. A single vet visit to address these itchy, persistent symptoms can cost $250 or more. And while that’s a fraction of what you might pay for pet insurance, costs of chronic conditions can add up over your pet’s lifetime.

Vet bills can also balloon quickly if chronic conditions require surgery. One example of this is hip dysplasia, a genetic disorder that can cause arthritis and pain in large-breed dogs. Depending on the procedure, you could pay up to $10,000 for hip surgery.

Many pet insurance plans cover treatments for hip dysplasia, including surgical costs, as long as it's not a pre-existing condition. However, some policies have waiting periods of six to 12 months for orthopedic issues like hip dysplasia. If symptoms develop during that waiting period, the condition will be considered pre-existing. That’s another argument for buying your pet’s coverage earlier rather than later.

Most importantly, having insurance means you can choose the best treatment for your pet no matter the cost. “It's really tragic when it's a life and death decision that is needed and it’s based on someone's financial constraints,” Greenstein says. “What is best and what's affordable often are not on the same side of the spectrum.”

One option to keep pet insurance affordable is to buy an accident-only plan. This type of coverage is usually much cheaper, costing about $190 annually for dogs and $112 for cats, according to NAPHIA. These plans cover injuries resulting from accidents, which include not just wounds and broken bones but also objects or toxins your pet might swallow.

However, an accident-only plan won’t help with some of the most common vet expenses. Illnesses, not injuries, are behind most claims submitted to pet insurer Lemonade, the company said in an email. In the past year, more than 60% of Lemonade's pet insurance claims stemmed from illnesses. That’s twice as many as the next-largest claim category, which was preventive care. A similar trend can be found in data from Nationwide, where chronic diseases make up a clear majority of pet insurance claims submitted to the company in 2025.

If you opt for an accident-only plan or no coverage at all, it’s still smart to set aside some savings for those inevitable pet emergencies and illnesses. This pool of funds can keep you from having to make difficult decisions about your pet’s care based solely on the cost of treatment.

#3056. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3054. MFA Financial (MFA) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Rate-driven losses dominated, but succession and new loan deals now matter most.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

MFA Financial, Inc. (MFA -0.45%) reported a GAAP net loss for the third quarter as sharp increases in treasury yields and wider mortgage spreads resulted in significant unrealized mark-to-market declines on its portfolio. Management reported that the company remains focused on transitioning leadership and resolving its legacy credit book while deploying capital into a higher-yielding environment where new acquisitions are averaging coupons of 7.8%. The company reported improved underlying earnings power, with distributable earnings prior to realized credit losses rising to $0.44 per share. CFO Mike Roper noted that although near-term headwinds exist from maturing interest rate swaps, the company has options to offset these impacts through the continued recycling of unproductive capital and utilization of a balance sheet capable of supporting more leverage.

Operator: Greetings, and welcome to the MFA Financial Third Quarter 2026 Financial Results Call. [Operator Instructions] As a reminder, this conference is being recorded. I would now like to turn the conference over to your host, Hal Schwartz, General Counsel. Thank you. You may begin.

Harold Schwartz: Thank you, operator, and good morning, everyone. The information discussed on this conference call today may contain or refer to forward-looking statements regarding MFA Financial, Inc., which reflect management's beliefs, expectations and assumptions as to MFA's future performance and operations. All forward-looking statements speak only as of the date on which they are made.
