For additional information regarding MFA's use of forward-looking statements, please see the relevant disclosure in the press release announcing MFA's third quarter 2026 financial results. Thank you for your time.

I would now like to turn the call over to MFA's CEO, Craig Knutson.

Craig Knutson: Thank you, Hal. Good morning, everyone, and thank you for joining us for MFA Financial's Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Call. With me today are Bryan Wulfsohn, our President and Chief Investment Officer; Mike Roper, our Chief Financial Officer; and other members of our senior management team. Before I get to the quarter, I'd like to say a few words about the announcement that we made on October 8. As you I'm sure saw, I plan to retire from my role as Chief Executive Officer at the end of June next year, and Bryan will become President and CEO on July 1, 2027.

Bryan will also join our Board on January 1 of next year, and I expect to remain on the Board after I retire, so I'm not going to disappear. This transition is the result of a deliberate succession process on which I have been working with our Board since early 2023. Bryan has run our residential whole loan portfolio since we began buying loans in 2014, and since late 2024 has overseen Lima One and our Agency MBS and BPL portfolios. Bryan has played an active and central role in shaping both the MFA of today and the strategy that guides us.

I've worked with him for over 20 years, both at MFA and previously, and I can't think of anyone better suited to lead the company than Bryan. We also announced that Andrew Kail will join us on January 1 as Chief Investment Officer. I've known Andrew for over 15 years, and he brings over 25 years of mortgage finance experience most recently as Head of Residential Lending at Starwood Capital. Andrew's arrival will let Bryan spend the first half of next year preparing to step into the CEO role, while our investment team gains the benefit of another very experienced leader with deep expertise in our target assets. Bryan will say more about this in a few minutes.

It's been a privilege to work at MFA for the last 18.5 years and to lead the company for the last 9. I'm sure I'll have more to say on that topic in the coming months. But for now, there's plenty of work to do, so let me turn to the quarter. The third quarter was a difficult one for rates, particularly in September. During the quarter, the market began to come to terms with a more hawkish Fed as inflation remains sticky and the economy and payrolls stayed strong. In September, the Fed raised the Fed funds rate by 25 basis points, their first increase since 2023, and the accompanying message made clear that more increases will likely follow.

Although the Fed left rates unchanged at their meeting last week, market expectations are overwhelmingly anticipating a hike at the December meeting. Treasury yields moved sharply higher in the third quarter. The 2-year yield rose over 70 basis points during the quarter from 4.17% to 4.89%. The 10-year rose almost 85 basis points from 4.46% to 5.29%, the highest level in 2 decades. The last time the 10-year closed this high was in 2007. Most of that move came in September and much of it in the final week.

Oil prices climbed with continued tension with Iran, rate volatility spiked with the MOVE index rising from the high 70s at the start of September to above 110 at month end, its largest 1-week jump since April of last year. Against that backdrop, credit spreads were relatively resilient, but mortgage spreads were not. Agency MBS underperformed meaningfully in September. Non-QM securitization spreads, which tightened into mid-September, widened back out to about 140 to 150 basis points on AAAs by quarter end. Mortgage rates rose above 7.25%, up about 50 basis points in September alone. We felt the impact of these moves.

Our portfolio carries interest rate risk that we hedge to an approximate 1-year duration gap, but the combination of a sharp increase in rates, wider mortgage spreads and swap rates that rose less than treasuries collectively worked against us. It is worth noting that most of the book value decline was due to unrealized losses from marks. Mike will walk through the drivers in more detail. While book value was disappointing, we continue to make progress on the operating priorities we laid out for you over the past year. First, we continue to work through our legacy credit book. Realized credit losses improved from the second quarter as we said they would.

Our overall portfolio 60-plus day delinquency rate improved again to 6.7% and our legacy multifamily portfolio shrank to under $300 million UPB and $263 million fair value. With a clear path to resolution on most of the remaining multifamily loans and most of these losses already reflected in DE, we believe our multifamily credit issues are largely behind us, particularly as we look forward to 2027. Second, the earnings power of the portfolio continued to improve. Distributable earnings rose to $0.26 per share from $0.12 in the second quarter and DE prior to realized credit losses was $0.44 per share. Third, we kept our expense base tight.

G&A for the quarter was $23.6 million, well below the $33 million quarterly average of 2024. Fourth, we continued issuing in the securitization market. We issued a new $447 million NPL securitization in September after calling our 2024 NPL deal, unlocking an additional $40 million of capital and issued a Non-QM deal shortly after quarter end. As we have discussed previously, the securitization markets are remarkably deep and resilient even in the face of general fixed income market volatility. None of this makes the third quarter book value decline easier to accept, and we still have more work to do.

We need to finish resolving the legacy credit book and turn improving earnings power into consistent results, but the same market that hurt us in the third quarter has also created opportunity. Loans that we acquired in Q3 carried an average coupon of 7.8%, and we expect returns on new investments today will produce mid- to high-teen ROEs. We will remain disciplined, but we will continue to put capital to work in this attractive investment environment. I'd like to turn the call over to Mike now to discuss our financial results.

Michael Roper: Thanks, Craig, and good morning, everyone. At September 30, GAAP book value was $11.12 per share and economic book value was $11.44 per share, down approximately 12.5% and 13.3%, respectively, from the end of the second quarter. MFA again paid a common dividend of $0.36 and delivered a quarterly total economic return of negative 10.6%. For the third quarter, we reported a GAAP net loss of $111.7 million or $1.19 per basic common share. The loss was driven by net mark-to-market declines of approximately $153 million across our various fair value assets, liabilities and hedges as rates rose sharply and mortgage spreads widened, particularly in late September.

Net interest income, including TBA dollar roll income, was $63.4 million, up from $60.2 million in the second quarter. Our G&A expenses declined $23.6 million, attributable primarily to nonrecurring depreciation expenses in the second quarter and a nonrecurring benefit from a true-up of incentive compensation accruals in the third quarter. Moving to Distributable earnings. DE for the quarter was $26.4 million or $0.26 per share, up from $0.12 per share in the second quarter. Realized credit losses on fair value loans declined to $18.9 million, consistent with the expectations we shared last quarter. DE prior to realized credit losses was $45.3 million or $0.44 per share.

That figure includes approximately $0.05 per share of nonrecurring benefits related to a refund of disputed servicing costs and a reduction of incentive compensation accruals in the third quarter. Excluding those items, DE prior to realized credit losses was approximately $0.39 per share, an increase of 11% from last quarter and 30% from the third quarter of 2025. This marks the third straight quarter of significant growth in the underlying earnings power of our portfolio and reflects the progress of the strategic initiatives that we shared a year ago.

In light of the significant market volatility over the last few months, we also want to provide an update on how we're thinking about the path of our distributable earnings from here. Earlier this year, we shared our expectations for elevated credit losses in 2026 as we work to resolve our legacy multifamily portfolio. This has played out largely as we expected. While these resolutions have been a drag on our DE, many of our most troubled legacy multifamily loans have now been resolved or charged off, and we continue to expect realized credit losses to moderate meaningfully from here.

As we look past the near-term, the recent move in rates and spreads has added significantly more variability to the longer-term outlook for our DE. We also have some known headwinds with approximately $1.3 billion of 1.4% pay fixed interest rate swaps that mature at the end of the year and into the first quarter of next year. These swaps contribute approximately $0.08 per share per quarter to our DE today. We have meaningful options to offset this loss carry and to continue to grow the portfolio's earnings power. With new investments coming on at returns in the mid- to high teens, the continued recycling of unproductive capital and a balance sheet capable of supporting significantly more leverage.

How quickly this builds will depend in part on how volatility, rates and markets evolve. As always, our Board will continue to evaluate the dividend each quarter, taking into account our long-term earnings outlook, taxable income, market conditions and other factors. Finally, subsequent to quarter end, we estimate that our economic book value has increased by approximately 5% since the end of September, driven by a rally in interest rates. I'd now like to turn the call over to Bryan, who will discuss our investment portfolio and Lima One.

Bryan Wulfsohn: Thanks, Mike. Before I get into the portfolio, I want to add a few words to what Craig said about our announcement last month. I'm honored that the Board has asked me to lead MFA, and I'm grateful to Craig, who has been a mentor and partner to me for more than 2 decades. We have a long runway to make this a smooth handoff next summer. I'm also very glad Andrew Kail is joining us in January. I've known Andrew for over 15 years. He has built and run residential lending businesses, and he knows the Non-QM business purpose and broader residential credit markets from both sides of the table.

As CIO, Andrew will lead our investment activities alongside the team that has been with us through many cycles. Andrew adds experience, expertise and relationships to our investment team, and we look forward to working with him. Now to the quarter. Despite a very volatile September, we continued to add assets, securitize loans, reduce our legacy credit exposure and make improvements at Lima One. I'll touch on each. Starting with Non-QM, which remains our largest asset class at nearly $6 billion. We acquired over $480 million of loans during the quarter with an average coupon of 7% and an LTV of 70%. These were sourced through a select group of long-standing origination partners.

Just after quarter end, we issued our 25th Non-QM securitization, successfully selling $360 million of bonds and locking in nonrecourse non-mark-to-market financing on the underlying loans. We also remain pleased with the performance of our seasoned RPL and NPL loans, which, as a reminder, were loans originated pre-GFC that were acquired at discounts to par between 2014 and 2019. The default rate in that portfolio fell to another all-time low during Q3. In September, we called our 2024 NPL securitization and issued $379 million of new bonds with an average coupon of 6%. That transaction unlocked $40 million in cash and financing capacity that we can utilize to make new investments.

Turning to our agency book, which comprises nearly 1/3 of our investment portfolio at roughly $4 billion. Though we did not sell any bonds due to volatile market conditions, we were active during the quarter, rotating lower coupon securities into higher coupon spec pools. We also continued to roll our $478 million TBA position. Agency spreads widened in September as rate volatility spiked, which hurt book value this quarter. But at current spreads, we believe agencies offer mid- to high teens ROEs and remain an attractive liquid complement to our credit assets. At Lima One, origination volume was $288 million in the third quarter, including $214 million of short-term transitional loans and $74 million of 30-year rental loans.

Although these figures were a slight drop from Q2, we have not and will not compromise the underwriting improvements we've made over the past 2 years in order to grow volume. We sold $95 million of rental loans during the quarter, generating $2.4 million of gain on sale income. We're pleased to announce that last week, Lima originated and distributed its first multifamily loans since the spring of 2024. This was the culmination of months of hard work by an entirely new underwriting regime. As with Lima's 30-year rental production, our business plan for multifamily will be originate to sell. Finally, we continue to wind down our legacy assets.

Our multifamily loan book fell below $300 million of remaining principal and the delinquent bucket fell to $71 million. As Mike noted, credit losses came in below the second quarter, and we expect them to moderate meaningfully from here. Across our entire loan portfolio, 60-plus delinquencies improved to 6.7% from 7% at June 30. Overall, while the rate environment made for a difficult quarter for book value, we were able to add assets at attractive levels, Lima continued to perform well and our problem asset book continued to shrink. And with that, we'll turn the call over to the operator for questions.

Bryan Wulfsohn: Yes. We just originated our first loans last week. Those amounted to around -- somewhere around $5 million in loans. We've seen improvement in that business even in the face of sort of moderating home prices.

#3053. The Profitability Play: Pricing Strategy For What's Next

​When markets become less predictable, pricing decisions can determine whether a company can sustain revenue growth. Government policy shifts, supply chain and energy disruptions, and aggressive competition can quickly change what customers are willing to pay and what it costs to serve them. Pricing strategies built around yesterday’s conditions can leave businesses reacting too slowly to protect profitability.

At the heart of every business lies an engine that drives revenue, profitability and customer engagement. And at the heart of that engine is the balance between the thing you build and the value it delivers, defined by the price.

Getting pricing right increases customer satisfaction while creating an agreement of value. In other words, it produces the profit that powers your future. Yet, many businesses fail to fully leverage this advantage.​

Unprofitable growth occurs when companies sacrifice too much margin to win business. Heavy discounting may help meet short-term revenue targets, but it leaves less profit to invest in product development and other building opportunities, all while competitors are supporting sustainable market position.

Much of the unpredictability is beyond the capacity of traditional planning since it comes from broad macro trends and the action of external parties. Organizations must embrace an economic and business landscape that’s defined not by punctuated periods of change but rather by systemic unpredictability that’s a permanent aspect of the global economy. The pricing strategy must become more responsive, more situationally aware and more resilient to sudden changes.​

Companies can no longer rely on expansion alone to succeed—pricing is the only business function that directly translates the value you create into the revenue you keep.

Yet, according to a Simon-Kucher global study, “only 40% of companies rank pricing as their top profit lever.” At the same time, the price realization rate (when businesses can actually get the prices they set) has fallen to 43% as buyers push back on pricing models and demand higher discounts and competitors compete aggressively for customers.​​

The gap between intended prices and realized prices is where businesses can lose profitability without fully understanding why. A company may have a well-defined pricing strategy on paper, but if sales teams routinely discount to close deals, pricing guidance arrives too late or changes go unnoticed, the intended results may never materialize.​

AI-based pricing technology can help companies respond more quickly, but its value depends on the quality of the information behind its recommendations and how effectively those recommendations reach the people making commercial decisions.

Data-driven pricing guidance can make a measurable difference in how sales teams negotiate. According to a 2024 study from Bain, 74% of sales representatives and managers with data-driven deal guidance said they were confident negotiating prices with customers.

For those businesses that are able to fully engage AI in their pricing approach, the results can be significant: In the same Bain study, businesses utilizing AI in this way reported a 12% higher win rate.

​​This need to engage pricing with business operations applies in both directions. Feeding pricing guidance to sellers at the point of interaction with a buyer helps drive up margins. And feeding win/loss data into your AI model creates pricing models that meet customer and market changes as they occur.

Building a pricing strategy that can provide profitable growth in even the most turbulent macroeconomic landscape is clearly a powerful competitive and financial tool.

There are, fundamentally, three key elements to achieving such a pricing strategy:

1. Businesses should start by identifying where price realization breaks down. Compare planned prices with actual transaction prices, then examine discount patterns by product, customer segment, sales team and deal outcome. This helps distinguish between discounts that are necessary to remain competitive and those that are eroding margins without meaningfully improving win rates.​​​

2. Use AI to accelerate pricing agility but do so with a clear view on data quality and provenance. Before deploying AI-driven pricing, assess whether the underlying data is accurate, current and representative of the customers being served.

3. Ensure the pricing strategy is operationally integrated into go-to-market functions. Assign clear ownership for reviewing that feedback and updating pricing guidance so the strategy evolves alongside the market.​

An effective and enabled pricing function is an essential part of a company’s overall commerce engine. This approach embeds pricing as part of a clear chain of commerce data and processes, helping businesses respond to changing markets and winning deals without sacrificing profit.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3051. Chase Will Add 10,000+ Vacation Homes to The Edit in 2027

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

You’ll also get an in-stay credit when you book a vacation home through The Edit for four nights or more.

Starting in 2027, Chase’s The Edit collection of upscale hotel and resort stays will broaden to include more than 10,000 vacation homes. If you carry the Chase Sapphire Reserve® or the Sapphire Reserve for Business℠, that means you’ll soon have more ways to use The Edit credits on those cards.

This addition will significantly expand The Edit’s footprint — currently at more than 1,500 hotels and resorts — although its usefulness will depend on your travel style. The new properties are curated in partnership with Plum Guide, a vacation-rental booking platform.

Plus, when you book a stay of four nights or more through The Edit, you’ll get a $150 in-stay credit to use at your vacation home.

Here’s everything you need to know about the upcoming changes to The Edit.

Eligible Chase cardholders can currently book vacation homes through the issuer’s travel portal. However, those bookings are not eligible to receive The Edit benefits. That changes Jan. 1, 2027, with select listings.

If you have the Chase Sapphire Reserve® or the Sapphire Reserve for Business℠, here’s what you can expect when you book a vacation home through The Edit:

Up to $250 in statement credits on prepaid stays of two nights or more. Chase Sapphire Reserve® cardholders are capped at $500 in credits a year, while Sapphire Reserve for Business℠ cardholders get up to $1,000 (starting Jan. 1, 2027).

$150 in-stay credit when booking a stay of at least four nights.

Early check-in and late checkout, when available.

Compared to booking hotels and resorts with The Edit, you’re losing out on perks like a $100 property credit (which doesn’t have a four-night minimum), complimentary breakfast and room upgrades.

While cardholders will be able to redeem points toward vacation-home stays, Chase told NerdWallet that these bookings will not be eligible for Points Boost at launch.

You’ll also need to book vacation homes at least seven days in advance — a requirement that’s currently in place with standard vacation homes available in Chase’s travel portal. Chase said it plans to reduce that requirement in the near future.

Chase’s addition of vacation homes to The Edit follows American Express’ recent move to add Airbnb Luxe properties to its own lodging credit for eligible cardholders.

When you book a vacation home for four nights or more through The Edit, you’ll get a $150 in-stay credit to use on services like massages, a private chef, dry cleaning and airport pick-ups.

Options vary by property, but you’ll see a list of available offerings prior to booking your stay. You won’t be able to see pricing, but you can reach out to a Reserve travel designer — a travel concierge who can help plan your trip — to get pricing estimates prior to booking. (You won’t be able to contact the vacation home host until after you’ve booked your stay.)

While it’s nice to get the credit, the four-night minimum could be frustrating for cardholders. When you book hotels through The Edit, there isn’t a stay minimum to get the $100 property credit (although you must stay two nights to use your eligible card’s $250 prepaid stay credit).

By comparison, AmEx offers a $250 Airbnb Experiences credit on the American Express Platinum Card® or The Business Platinum Card® from American Express each calendar year when you book a prepaid Airbnb Luxe property through its travel portal. Terms apply. That statement credit is good for the rest of the calendar year in which the booking is confirmed. However, you can only unlock one $250 credit a year, even if you make multiple bookings.

Booking a vacation home through The Edit makes the most sense for longer group trips.

One of the best perks of booking a vacation home is that everyone gets to stay together in a shared space — something that’s difficult to do in a traditional hotel. If you’re able to get a larger group, you’ll also have more people to split the booking cost with.

Plus, you’ll want to stay at least four nights to benefit from the $150 in-stay credit.

If you’re traveling by yourself or with a partner on a shorter trip, you might find more value staying at a hotel or resort that’s part of The Edit. You’ll get useful perks like complimentary breakfast for two and a $100 property credit.

Ultimately, the biggest benefit of adding vacation homes to The Edit is creating more booking options. If vacation homes fit your travel style, this is a great development to look forward to. If not, you’ll still be able to book hotels through The Edit like before.

#3049. 20 Must-Have Security Controls When Outsourcing Sensitive Workloads

As companies rely more heavily on third-party providers for infrastructure and computing capacity, they’re increasingly placing sensitive applications and data in environments outside their direct control. When workloads involve confidential information or critical business operations, relying on outside providers creates additional security and oversight challenges. A provider’s security assurances alone aren’t enough to guarantee that company data and systems remain protected.

Before moving these workloads outside their own environments, organizations need to consider which safeguards will help them maintain visibility and control while protecting sensitive information. Below, members of Forbes Technology Council share the controls companies should insist on before entrusting sensitive workloads to third-party providers.

Companies should work to ensure they are clear on the contractual terms associated with the use of these external computing environments, especially as they relate to access to environments and data, usage terms remaining exclusive to the owner/operator, and provisions about backups and other redundancy practices and methods. These practices have a history but require some modernization to ensure risk is managed in the current era of external reliance. - Charlie Sanchez, Black & Veatch

Insist on verifiable control over where data and models run and who can touch them. Before moving sensitive workloads, require customer-managed encryption keys, no training on your data, and audit logs you can inspect. In healthcare, that means a HIPAA-compliant BAA and proof that PHI never leaves your approved environment. Trust, but verify continuously. - David Talby, John Snow Labs

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Ensure that there is a tested ability to revoke third-party access, rather than just a contractual obligation. Implement a gate for QE acceptance by ensuring that revocation denies access to sensitive information by active sessions and automated processes, even when the support team of the provider cannot be reached. - Kavitha Thiyagarajan, Newrez LLC

Always insist on holding your own encryption keys outside the provider’s environment. Contracts and certifications describe what a provider promises, but key custody is something you can verify and enforce yourself. If you control the keys, a provider breach, a legal demand or a change of jurisdiction is far less likely to expose your data without you. This is especially important in a sovereign compliance context. - Paul Speciale, Scality

Organizations must insist on absolute data sovereignty and immutable auditability, not just basic access controls. With AI-driven processing, companies need explicit zero-data-retention guarantees, customer-controlled encryption keys, and complete traceability over how data is processed. Sensitive workloads must never be used for background model training or vendor analytics, with real-time audit logs and instant access revocation built in by default. - Chris Brown, Sngular

Insist on a way to run without the vendor. The system should run inside your own cloud or an isolated environment so that personal identifiers arrive scrambled and the vendor never holds the key. Then, add source code in escrow with an independent agent and a license that keeps you running if the vendor fails. If the vendor can switch you off or read your data, the workload is not really yours. - Vivek Thomas, AISensum

Before moving sensitive workloads, require proof that the provider can isolate your data and limit privileged access. Review independent testing, access logs and remediation practices—not just policy documents. If the provider cannot demonstrate that those controls operate effectively and are retested after material changes, the workload should not move. - Brian Harmison, Corsica Technologies

Insist on an architecture that minimizes data movement. Sensitive data should stay in the company’s environment whenever possible, with only the metadata or security findings needed by the provider shared externally. Every additional copy creates another retention, residency and access problem that has to be secured. - Asaf Kochan, Sentra

Before moving sensitive workloads to a third party, companies should insist on continuous visibility and policy enforcement. It’s not enough to trust security certifications. Organizations need real-time insight into where data resides, who accesses it, how it’s used and whether it meets compliance requirements. Strong controls, auditability and zero-trust principles ensure risks are detected and managed before they become business or regulatory issues. - Hemant Soni, CAPGEMINI AMERICA INC.

Demand a clear “kill switch.” If a vendor is hosting sensitive workloads, your company should be able to quickly cut off access, revoke credentials, and move or isolate data if something goes wrong. Trust is useful, but control over your own exit is what really protects you. - Margarita Simonova, ILoveMyQA

I’d want a very clear understanding of access. Who can reach the workload, what can they do, and how quickly can that access be removed? As systems become more connected, small gaps in permissions can create much larger risks. Clear ownership and tightly managed access are worth establishing before sensitive workloads move anywhere. - Deniz Gezgin, Eightpoint

Security and assurance mean knowing a provider won’t misuse your data or intellectual property to build a competing product. This was a major concern when AWS, Azure and Google entered the cloud market. Companies feared providers would learn from what they built and compete with them. The cloud companies were careful not to cross that line. With AI, we’re seeing the same concern return, and some frontier AI companies are crossing it. - Srinivas Mukkamala, Securin Inc.

Strong vendor management is essential, but if I could only ask for one thing, it would be a SOC 2 report. It’s not a complete security assessment, but it provides some of the best security and control visibility available from a single document—provided you actually review it. - Shane O’Donnell, Centric Consulting

Companies should insist on legally binding agreements requiring third-party providers to comply with their data processing governance framework. These agreements must define data ownership, permitted processing activities, security standards, access controls, audit rights and accountability for breaches. Outsourcing infrastructure should not mean surrendering control over sensitive data or responsibility for its governance. - Eranda Maldeniya, Enterprise Analytics

Insist on the right to audit and the right to get your data back, in usable form, on exit. Performance SLAs are table stakes; the real control is what happens when the relationship ends or the provider is breached. In regulated work, you answer to the regulator for data you don’t hold. If you can’t inspect it and can’t leave with it, you don’t control it. - Mayur Khandelwal, EXL

Ask for the list of non-human identities on their side that can touch your workload. Every provider talks about which humans have admin access. Nobody volunteers the service accounts, monitoring agents and automation inside their environment that read your data as part of normal operations. If they can’t produce that list, the control conversation hasn’t started yet. - Nidhi Jain, CloudEagle.ai

Make remote attestation a go-live requirement for sensitive workloads. Before deployment, teams should verify that the workload is running in an approved environment, with the expected code and access controls intact. If that proof cannot be produced, the workload should not move outside the enterprise. - Dr. Chiranjiv Roy, C5i.ai

Know exactly what happens to your data once it leaves your environment. Before adopting a third-party service, require clear documentation on where data is processed, how long it is retained, who can access it, and whether it can be used for secondary purposes, including AI model training. Make these requirements enforceable through contractual terms. - Anastasia Georgievskaya, Haut.AI

Insist on a joint incident drill before anything moves. The contract and the SOC 2 report tell you what is supposed to happen. A tabletop with the provider’s on-call team shows you who actually picks up, how fast you get logs, and who can isolate your workload. If a provider won’t rehearse with you, find out why before you sign. - Ganesh Ariyur, Transform Smarter

Insist on a specific incident notification timeline written into the contract before any sensitive workload moves. Most third-party agreements include breach notification language that is vague on timing and structured to protect the vendor. A contractual obligation to notify within a defined window, regardless of whether the provider has finished its own investigation, determines whether you can respond or can only react. - Dan Haiem, AppMakers USA

→ Baca artikel asli

Peloton Interactive Inc. forecast holiday-quarter revenue that was slightly above Wall Street’s expectations, even as the fitness company projected a year-over-year sales decline and narrower profit margins.

#3047. Why Rare Earth Stock MP Materials Dropped 11.9% in October

Temperatures fell last month -- so did the market's enthusiasm for this rare-earth leader.

After falling 13.6% in September, MP Materials (MP -5.08%) shares continued to tumble last month. With firms curbing their enthusiasm for the rare-earth stock, investors had sufficient cause to click the sell button.

According to data provided by S&P Global Market Intelligence, MP Materials stock fell 11.9% in October.

Maintaining an outperform rating, Baird cut its price target 19% to $65 from $80 on Oct. 1. Based on MP Materials stock closing at $47.29 on Sept. 30, Baird's new price target implied upside of 37%.

A few days later, Morgan Stanley adopted a similar perspective on MP Materials stock, slashing its price target to $66 from $73. According to Thefly.com, Morgan Stanley stated in a note on Americas Metals & Mining that it currently prefers precious metal stocks, recognizing "upside to spot gold and silver prices near-term and higher long-term price forecasts."

Later in October, Goldman Sachs also adopted a less optimistic stance, lowering its price target on MP Materials to $57 from $65, while maintaining a buy rating.

MP Materials is an American company that operates throughout the rare-earth supply chain. In addition to owning and operating the Mountain Pass Rare Earth Mine and Processing Facility in California, the company owns and operates Independence, a rare-earth metal, alloy, and magnet manufacturing facility in Texas.

The company has two reportable segments. The materials segment generates revenue primarily from sales of neodymium-praseodymium oxide and metal, mostly sold to customers in the United States, Japan, and South Korea. The magnetics segment includes the Independence facility, which produces and sells magnetic precursor products and rare-earth permanent magnets.

It's not only the last week of October that was challenging for MP Materials. Shares of the rare-earth specialist are currently down about 53% from their 52-week high. While the recent plunge may be alarming, the company hasn't reported anything disastrous that underpins the drop. Instead, it's simply part of a sectorwide decline in rare-earth stocks.

For those who have been waiting patiently for MP Materials stock to dip to a more attractive entry point, now may be a great time to act. Before doing so, though, investors will want to dig into the company's third-quarter 2026 financial results (expected to be released after the market closes on Nov. 5) to confirm that it's making progress with construction of its 10X facility, a large rare-earth magnet manufacturing facility located in Texas.

Scott Levine has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Goldman Sachs Group and MP Materials. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3046. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3044. SpaceX vs. Rocket Lab vs. AST SpaceMobile: 3 Space Stocks Compared on the Only Numbers That Matter

The three companies face different cash flow challenges and valuation risks that could shape investor returns.

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -3.81%), also called SpaceX, reported revenue of about $14.6 billion in the third quarter. However, more than half came from its Super Intelligence (SI) business, formerly known as the artificial intelligence (AI) business, rather than from its traditional space operations. SpaceX's other two businesses include Space, which manufactures and launches reusable rockets, and Connectivity, which provides satellite internet services through Starlink.

Rocket Lab (RKLB -3.79%) generated $234 million in its fiscal second quarter (ended June 30), mainly from its Space Systems segment. Rocket Lab manufactures spacecraft and satellite components through Space Systems, while its Launch Services business sends satellites and other payloads into space.

AST SpaceMobile (ASTS -4.12%) reported just $31.5 million in its fiscal second quarter (ended June 30), with no recognized revenue from its planned satellite-cellular service. AST SpaceMobile is developing a satellite-based cellular broadband network that aims to connect ordinary smartphones directly to satellites through existing mobile operators.

However, revenue alone cannot determine which stock offers better returns. The more important numbers are the companies' ability to generate future cash flows relative to their current valuations.

SpaceX's Connectivity segment earned $2.37 billion in operating profit, while the SI segment earned $431 million in the third quarter. However, SI is also accounted for about $40 billion of the company's $48.1 billion in capital expenditures during the first nine months of 2026. SpaceX also reported $13.1 billion of operating cash flow and nearly $35 billion of negative free cash flow in the first nine months of 2026.

SpaceX's profitable Connectivity business is not yet generating enough cash to offset the company's enormous AI infrastructure spending. Additionally, a single unidentified SI customer accounted for 34.5% of the company's third-quarter revenue, equivalent to roughly two-thirds of SI revenue. The high customer concentration risk makes future cash generation particularly sensitive to that customer's spending decisions.

Rocket Lab and AST SpaceMobile are also free cash flow negative. In the first six months of 2026, Rocket Lab reported about $188 million in negative free cash flow, compared with roughly $1 billion for AST SpaceMobile.

AST SpaceMobile is burning cash at a high rate mainly due to investment in its satellite components and supporting infrastructure. While Rocket Lab needs to improve cash generation from its existing businesses and future launches, AST SpaceMobile must still convert its network investments into recurring commercial service revenue.

Rocket Lab exited the second quarter with an order backlog of $2.36 billion, of which 45.5% is expected to convert into revenue by June 2027. This provides visibility into future sales, although revenue still depends on the fulfillment of launch and spacecraft contracts. The inaugural launch of the company's next-generation Neutron rocket has been pushed to 2027, potentially delaying revenue from the new rocket while development costs continue.

Rocket Lab's planned acquisition of Iridium Communications could add an established satellite-services business. Iridium Communications reported $245.7 million of revenue in the third quarter, with services accounting for 76% of the total.

However, certain risks can't be ignored. In September, Rocket Lab raised $1.94 billion by issuing 29.3 million shares, primarily to finance the acquisition. Iridium Communications' existing term loans, totaling about $1.78 billion as of June 30, are also expected to remain outstanding after the deal closes, with a guarantee from Rocket Lab's operating subsidiary.

Hence, the acquisition will create value for existing shareholders only if the additional cash flow justifies the equity dilution and increasing financial obligations.

AST SpaceMobile believes it has sufficient funding to manufacture and launch about 90 satellites. However, deploying the network is only part of the challenge. The company must also generate enough recurring revenue to cover operating costs and earn attractive returns on its investment.

SpaceX, Rocket Lab, and AST SpaceMobile traded at 57 times, 40.8 times, and 101.8 times trailing-12-month sales (as of Oct. 30), respectively. These premium valuations already assume significant ramp-up in revenue and profit in the coming quarters.

AST SpaceMobile's valuation is particularly steep. The company expects $150 million to $200 million in revenue for 2026, supported partly by U.S. government contracts. Hence, even if AST SpaceMobile achieves the upper end of this forecasts, its $13 billion market capitalization would still represent more than 61 times projected 2026 revenue.

AST SpaceMobile also expects to recognize only 6.6% of its $1.2 billion in remaining contractual performance obligations as revenue by June 2027. This highlights that a significant amount of time is required to convert contracted business into recognized revenue. With its commercial SpaceMobile service yet to generate recognized revenue, AST SpaceMobile's valuation depends heavily on future network monetization.

Rocket Lab needs to become free cash flow positive, while SpaceX must demonstrate that its substantial AI investments can deliver attractive returns.

Of the three, Rocket Lab appears to be the best pick on a risk-reward basis, although its valuation is rich. The company's established launch and spacecraft businesses provide a foundation for growth, while the Iridium acquisition could add recurring service revenue. However, the company must demonstrate that its expansion can improve per-share cash flow after accounting for dilution and financing obligations.

SpaceX ranks second. Starlink's profitability is a significant advantage, but the company's huge AI investments can't be ignored.

AST SpaceMobile ranks third for now. The company's satellite-cellular network offers substantial growth potential, but commercial service revenue and long-term profitability remain unproven.

Manali Pradhan, CFA has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends AST SpaceMobile and Rocket Lab. The Motley Fool recommends Space Exploration Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3042. CEO Andy Jassy Just Told Investors That AWS Can Grow Revenue 40% in 2027 and 2028 and Eventually Become a Trillion-Dollar Business. Is It Enough to Get Wall Street on Board?

Despite strong guidance for AWS over the next few years, Amazon sold off following its recent earnings report.

Although investors are extremely worried about an artificial intelligence bubble and when it might pop, it's also become clear that the market has significantly raised its threshold for evaluating large hyperscaler and AI stocks. As of this writing, the Roundhill Magnificent Seven ETF has declined more than 6% over the past month, despite many earnings reports showing signs that AI is benefiting their businesses.

For instance, on Amazon's (AMZN +6.12%) third-quarter earnings call, CEO Andy Jassy told analysts that he expects the company's cloud business, Amazon Web Services (AWS), to grow revenue by more than 40% in both 2027 and 2028, becoming a trillion-dollar business at some point in the next five to 10 years.

Is this enough for Wall Street to get on board?

Amazon CEO Andy Jassy. Image source: Getty Images.

It's hard to look at Amazon's third-quarter earnings and say AI isn't significantly benefiting the business. AWS experienced 39.3% year-over-year revenue growth, the highest level seen in nearly five years. Revenue also achieved an annualized run rate of $184 billion.

AWS benefits from AI in several ways, including selling compute capacity, renting out its custom chips, and selling access to AI models, tools, and agents.

In the third quarter, AWS accounted for nearly 23% of the company's total revenue but nearly 64% of its total operating profit, so AWS has by far the best margins of the three business lines.

AWS was Amazon's traditional cloud business, but it's clear that AI has become the big driver moving forward.

On the earnings call, Jassy told investors, "Enterprises are still very early in using inference at scale, and yet inference already accounts for well over half of AWS's AI revenue and is growing, and we have a lot of training revenue as well."

Furthermore, the AWS backlog is now $578 billion, driven by strong demand for core infrastructure and generative AI services.

The day after its earnings report, Amazon stock actually fell.

Investors remain concerned about the high spending required to achieve these growth rates at AWS. Amazon continues to guide for $220 billion in capital expenditures this year. Jassy also told analysts the company expects to spend "meaningfully more in 2027."

This intense level of spending has resulted in negative free cash flow of $42.4 billion for the company over the past 12 months, which has concerned the market.

Interestingly, Wall Street believes AWS's growth will materialize. Consensus estimates project at least 40% growth in AWS revenue in 2027 and 2028, according to S&P Global Market Intelligence. On Amazon's third-quarter earnings call, Jassy also came across as increasingly bullish about AWS's ability to generate compelling returns.

"We continue to accelerate our capex payback and have even more conviction than we already had that the investment we're making in AWS will yield a much larger revenue and free cash flow-generating business than we would have imagined a couple of years ago, with strong return on invested capital," Jassy said, adding that he views AI as "a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity." "We have a compelling flywheel spinning in AWS."

One reason the earnings report may not have received the response the company was looking for is that quarterly year-over-year growth expectations had climbed as high as 44% before the quarter, according to analysts at William Blair.

The real issue may simply be the unknown. While AI shows no signs of slowing, safety concerns have mounted, leading investors to wonder what could happen to a major AI company or the AI trade if a major security breach or safety event occurs.

Furthermore, investors also worry that AI might cannibalize existing businesses. For Amazon, there are concerns about how AI agents could impact Amazon's massive e-commerce and ads business.

The concerns might be overblown, but it's really difficult to imagine what AI looks like in the future. Meanwhile, companies like Amazon are spending hundreds of billions on AI, making it a make-or-break bet.

That's why expectations are so high -- because of the risks. Surely, if Amazon executes as Jassy says, the stock will benefit. But it's hard for investors to look that far ahead right now, even if they believe in the company's ability to capitalize on AI.

Bram Berkowitz has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3039. AI Is Agriculture's Next Big Opportunity. But Which Decisions Has It Actually Changed?

Aidan Connolly, President, AgriTech Capital, is a food/feed/farm futurologist and author of the book The Future of Agriculture.

​In a new survey of nearly 2,000 agri-food professionals, I asked where they see the greatest opportunities for AI and how they are using it today. The respondents were primarily agribusiness executives, consultants, technology providers and researchers, so the findings reflect industry perspectives.

Overall, their answers were unsurprising: 67% identified better decision-making as a major opportunity, and 65% pointed to productivity. However, the picture became more interesting when we asked about specific applications. Just 5% identified autonomous equipment and robotics as potentially the most significant application, while 31% selected decision support and recommendations.

These findings raise an important question: How does agriculture move from recognizing AI’s potential to using it in ways that genuinely improve business performance? The answer may have less to do with choosing the right technology than with identifying the decisions worth improving.

Twenty-nine percent of the respondents say that they have used AI features built into software they already owned, compared with 9% who had used a tool developed specifically for agriculture. That number suggests two things: First, it may undercount actual usage, as AI features are now tightly bundled into office software. More importantly, it indicates an opportunity for increased use of AI tools in both commercial and production decision making, and that the biggest AI opportunity for agribusiness right now may be in purchasing, inventory, scheduling or other commercial decisions.

McKinsey’s 2026 global survey, The State of AI: On the Road to ROI, found that 80% said AI had improved their individual productivity, while 37% attributed at least some EBIT impact to AI use. Although only 6% were classified as AI “high performers,” nearly 75% of those had fundamentally redesigned workflows because of AI (versus 25% of the other respondents).

Using AI to perform an existing task faster is different from redesigning how work gets done, but the latter is much more closely associated with meaningful returns. It also gets harder when the recommendation affects a biological or operational outcome in food and farming.

Generating recommendations about feed, treatment or nitrogen timing is very different from creating a document. The recommendation has to be specific enough to act on, providing the right information, at the right time.

Over the past two months, I’ve moderated conference panels in China, the U.K. and the U.S. At each, I’ve asked a simple question: Has AI actually changed a task or a decision in your business? Very few people have been prepared to say yes.

That gap between AI’s potential and actual impact matters particularly in agriculture. Saving time drafting an email is useful. Changing a nutrition, treatment or crop-management decision is a different test: Someone must act on the recommendation and live with the result. The University of Wisconsin-Madison asked 44 independent soybean experts to evaluate AI-generated management plans and found that the recommendations missed important regional differences. The conclusion was that agricultural AI needs to move toward tailored, field-specific advice before farmers can just trust it.

Adoption will partly depend on the user trusting recommendations enough to act on them. McKinsey’s Global Farmer Insights 2026 survey of 5,500 farmers across 10 countries found that just 17% are using AI for farm-related tasks, and only 6% cited generative AI or AI search as a trusted source for decision-making. Our respondents raised similar concerns: Integration with existing systems and accuracy were cited as serious concerns by 32% of respondents. Only lack of knowledge or training was seen as a more serious obstacle, at 52%.

McKinsey’s The State of AI in 2025 survey found that inaccuracy was the most common AI-related risk, and that high-performing organizations were more likely to have defined when model outputs need human validation. Our survey did not ask who should have the final say, but my view is simple: If an AI recommendation can affect an animal, a crop or the bottom line, somebody still needs to own the decision. Trust shouldn’t depend on how confidently AI delivers a recommendation. It should depend on how consistently those recommendations perform against the alternatives, and on knowing when human judgment is still required. Its track record should determine how much responsibility it earns.​

So where should an agribusiness begin? With the decisions, not the software. I’ve argued before that AI pilots should address specific business challenges, and the same principle applies here. Which recurring decisions have the greatest impact on margins: feed rations, treatment protocols, input purchasing, production scheduling? Who makes those decisions today, what information do they use and what would a wrong call cost? That last question really matters.

Start with a decision whose outcome can be evaluated relatively quickly, rather than one you can only judge at harvest. Choose one where the available information is reasonably good and the cost of error is manageable. Test the AI recommendation against the existing approach, identify who has authority to accept or override it and keep a record of what was recommended, what was done and what happened.

Then evaluate the results. Was the recommendation accurate? Was it used? Did it improve the outcome compared with the existing process? Building confidence will generally take repeated testing, but this is how experimentation becomes evidence.

Our survey respondents identified lack of knowledge and training as the biggest barrier to adoption. Their open-ended responses reinforced the need for practical training, better integration, confidence in accuracy and clearer evidence of value.

Before you ask which AI tool your business needs, ask which decision you most want to improve. What does it need to know, and how will you tell a season from now whether the recommendation was right? If you cannot answer those questions about the decision, buying another AI tool will not solve the problem.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3032. What Republicans' huge loss means for Trump

Democrats just put President Donald Trump in a bind.

The party is projected to win commanding majorities in the House and Senate after thrashing Republicans in key races up and down the ballot across the country. The blue tsunami will knock Washington on its head and crush the Republican congressional bulwark that both supported and shielded Trump through the first two years of his term.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The midterm result snuffs out any chance Trump had of passing partisan legislation in the second half of his presidency. And many of the interests he has been able to pursue without Congress' input — including tariffs, the Iran war and an array of Washington-area construction projects — are sure to face sudden pushback from newly empowered Democratic majorities.

The paradigm shift hardly leaves Trump helpless, however. As president at a time of executive power expansion, Trump wields singular authorities that could stymie Democrats' efforts to pursue their own agendas.

But with his grip on the Capitol shriveling and his approval ratings scraping record lows in numerous polls, it's unclear how much support he'll be able to muster to fight the coming political battles.

Even before Election Day, analysts and pundits had contemplated a Democratic rout so decisive that it would force a reckoning for the GOP, and potentially even pose an existential threat to Trump's "Make America Great Again" movement.

Marc Short, chairman of Advancing American Freedom and onetime chief of staff to former Vice President Mike Pence, told CNBC on Tuesday afternoon that the GOP has moved away from its conservative foundations.

"We need a refresh right now," Short said in an interview. "We've lost our way."

Trump has been vague about what he hopes to achieve with the rest of his term. But he has shared a vision of how he might coexist with a Democratic majority, and it involves heavy use of the veto pen.

"It's one of the stronger powers that a president has," Trump said in a Fox News interview overnight. "They'll send over things that are insane, and I'll veto them."

"And after a couple of months of vetoes, they'll call and they'll say, 'How do we get this stuff done?' And we'll work together," he said, adding, "I have a lot of friends that are Democrats."

Trump has previously stated his willingness to veto reams of legislation that Democrats send across his desk. In a New York Post podcast interview released Monday, Trump said that if Republicans don't win, "I veto everything."

Pressed to detail his plans for the next two years, Trump touted his record and said, "The most important thing is to keep it going. It's going to get better and better and better, unless people come in that hate our country."

If that happens, "I guess I'll become Mr. Veto," Trump said.

He may soon get his chance: House Democrats, poised to be led by current Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York, are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned.

The legislative push, first reported by The New York Times, aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to introduce bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law earlier this year.

Democrats also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the war with Iran, or seek congressional authorization to keep it going.

But without Trump's approval, those bills may end up as little more than symbolic gestures, as Democrats will lack the two-thirds majorities in both chambers that are required to override a presidential veto without GOP help.

And there's no guarantee the bills will even make it out of Congress: Despite their landslide victories, Senate Democrats will still end up shy of the 60 votes required to bypass the chamber's filibuster rules for most legislation, giving Republicans key leverage in the minority.

Democrats also could feasibly benefit from Trump's vetoes in the long term. Forcing the president to shoot down numerous popular bills could give them fodder in the 2028 election.

It's far from clear whether Trump believes he can find compromise with Democrats on any of their initial legislative priorities.

But any potential for a working relationship with the opposition could be poisoned early on, as Democrats in both chambers are aggressively signaling their plans to launch politically charged investigations against Trump, his political and corporate allies and even his family members.

Winning control of House committees gives Democrats the ability to issue subpoenas under the current rules.

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., who is likely to chair the House Oversight Committee in the next Congress, said his panel will target the Trump family's business dealings, corporate corruption and alleged abuses by federal immigration agencies. He also plans to return focus to the Trump administration's handling of files related to notorious sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, Garcia summed up his plans in a two-word statement: "Lawyer up."

It's not just Garcia. Other Democrats have already demanded records from the Trump administration on a range of issues. Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., for instance, last week accused Trump of using tariff exemptions to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

Senate Democrats have sent similar signals.

"They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we'll be printing," Sen. Ed Markey, D-Mass., said in an X post late Tuesday in response to a post, appearing to refer to tech executives.

The investigations are widely expected to consume much of the rest of Trump's term.

Trump has ample experience being targeted by Democrats, who impeached him twice in the House during his first presidential term.

But with his base of support appearing to erode inside and outside of Washington, Trump may have to face his political foes with a smaller arsenal than he's used to.

"I'm not expecting Republicans to revolt" against Trump following the midterm losses, AAF's Short told CNBC. "But I'm expecting some of them to quietly distance themselves."

#3031. I've Been Writing About Disney Stock for 30 Years. Here's Why My Conviction Has Never Been Higher.

I've had a lot to say about Disney over the past three decades. I'm just saying it louder now.

This might not be a good time to don my mouse ears. Shares of Walt Disney (DIS +4.18%) are barely trading higher this year. The stock has lost roughly a third of its value over the past five years. I still think that now is a great time to warm up to the leading media and entertainment company.

I've been a Disney shareholder for 40 years. I have now been contributing content to The Motley Fool for 31 years. I wrote about Disney then. I'm writing about Disney now. My conviction has never been higher. The combination of Disney's pipeline across several categories and its compelling forward valuation makes it a star you can wish upon again.

Disney's business is faring a lot better than its flat stock performance over the past year. Its operations and ugly chart since peaking in early 2021 are passing ships. Disney continues to oversee the world's most visited theme parks. According to last week's TEA Global Experiences Index -- an annual third-party research report that spells out attendance trends -- Disney had nine of the 11 most visited theme parks in the world. Closer to home, it operates the only five theme parks to attract more than 10 million turnstile clicks apiece in the U.S. in 2025.

It's not just dominant in gated attractions. U.S. movie studios cranked out just six theatrical releases in 2024 and 2025 that topped $1 billion in box office receipts. Disney was responsible for all of them. On the broadcast media front, Disney has written the playbook on navigating the transition from linear to digital distribution. Disney+ -- along with Hulu and ESPN -- has been collectively profitable for two years. Growth there is more than offsetting the declines of its original cable and over-the-air broadcasting businesses.

The future is bright, pretty much everywhere you look. Even in a rare category where it's not the dominant player, Disney is coasting. Disney isn't the largest player in cruise ships, but its ability to charge a premium for its family-friendly experience has made the top dogs envious for years. No one is growing its fleet faster -- on a percentage basis -- than the Houseboat of Mouse.

The past is historic, and not just because Disney is a master storyteller. The future could be an even better tale to tell. Its theme parks worldwide are expanding, adding bar-raising experiences that will open in the coming years. It raised admission prices for its domestic attractions last month. This happens every year, but folks keep coming. The elasticity is mesmerizing.

The same thing can be said about its streaming services. Disney+ and Hulu raised their monthly subscription rates in September. The same ad-free version of Disney+ that launched just seven years ago for $6.99 a month now costs $21.49 a month. Viewers are still glued to their screens.

What about the biggest screen that Disney serves? Your local multiplex is going to get a healthy dose of Disney product in the near future. Marvel's Avengers: Doomsday hits theaters next month. In a big year for exhibitors, this will be the highest-grossing movie of 2026. The spigot will keep gushing in 2027, with new Avengers, Frozen, and Star Wars movies coming out.

On the cruising front, the water continues to be inviting. Disney will add four more cruise ships in the next four years, each one with its own distinctive features that keep regulars coming back for more bon voyages.

Disney stock has been discarded like Jessie in Toy Story 2. It's expected to deliver its strongest revenue growth in more than three years when it announces its fiscal first-quarter results later this month. The bottom line will grow even faster. With momentum building and the future promising, you would expect to pay a juicy premium for your chunk of cheese in this Mickey Mouse company. Disney is cheaper than you think.

The shares are trading at less than 16 times what analysts expect it to earn for fiscal 2027, which began last month. Its 1.3% dividend yield may not seem like much, but it's approaching a historic high with its recent aggressive payout hikes.

There will always be risks when it comes to Disney. It's susceptible to a softening economy. There's a new CEO this year. There will be duds between the blockbusters. I'm comfortable with all of that. Quality matters, and there's a reason why Disney is dominant across many markets.

Rick Munarriz has positions in Walt Disney. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Walt Disney. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3027. Blood banks targeted in latest European fridge cyberattacks

French blood banks were on Monday affected by the recent wave of cybersecurity attacks on European refrigeration systems, the French Blood Establishment confirmed on Wednesday.

The organization said that while it wasn't the direct target of the attack, cold storage units operated by external providers containing the EFS' blood products were compromised across three of its sites, with hackers cranking up temperatures.

"Specifically, temperature and defrost thresholds were set to excessively high levels," the EFS said in a statement translated from French to English by CNBC.

"These cold storage units—located at EFS preparation and distribution sites—are used to store blood products prior to their delivery to healthcare facilities, as well as samples intended for EFS biobanks."

They also stored frozen plasma used for transfusions the manufacturing of medicinal products.

The EFS said that its surveillance systems were triggered rapidly, and depending on the situation, affected equipment was either brought under control with no impact on the products or samples, or they were taken offline. No samples or blood products were lost during the attack, with samples quickly transferred to other facilities, the EFS added.

The EFS is now coordinating with the French National Cybersecurity Agency (ANSSI) and the Ministry of Health and has increased monitoring of its equipment

Hundreds of supermarkets across France and other European countries, including Belgium, Sweden and Finland, had their fridges remotely hacked and temperatures raised, resulting in potentially millions of euros of spoiled food.

"Several regional facilities of the French Blood ​Establishment (EFS) were affected by a cybersecurity incident ​that impacted certain cold storage room monitoring and control ‌systems,"

The situation is now under control and is being closely monitored, the French Health Ministry said in a statement, according to Reuters. They added that the safety of the blood supplies was ​preserved "thanks ​to the ⁠facilities' monitoring and alarm systems, as well as precautionary measures immediately ​implemented."

#3024. Warriors’ Steve Kerr compares Steph Curry to NBA GOAT in late stages of career

From being a part of one of the greatest basketball teams ever in the 1990s as a player with the Bulls to coaching one of the greatest basketball dynasties in the 2010s with the Warriors, Steve Kerr knows what greatness looks like.

The Warriors coach appeared on ESPN's "NBA Tip-off" and told longtime host Ernie Johnson how coaching Steph Curry at age 38 reminds him of playing alongside Michael Jordan.

Steve Kerr reveals watching Steph Curry during late stages of career reminds him of Michael Jordan. AP Photo/David Zalubowski

"Honestly, it really does remind me of what it was like to play with Michael Jordan back in the day," Kerr said. "Every single night you just are amazed, and you marvel at what he's capable of doing no matter what the defense does but also just the longevity of it."

Last season, Curry appeared in only 43 games for the Warriors after spending most of the season on the team's bench due to patellofemoral pain syndrome, also known as runner's knee.

This season, Curry has played in six games and is averaging 28.8 minutes per game and is looking like his regular self.

Known as one of the greatest shooters of this era, Curry is averaging 30 points per game, shooting 42.9% from beyond the arc and 46.8% from the field.

Golden State Warriors coach Steve Kerr compares Steph Curry to NBA all-time great. NBAE via Getty Images

In his last three games, Curry has averaged more than 30 points, including a 44-point performance against the Timberwolves on Oct. 28.

"To see night after night, defenses doing everything possible to slow Steph down like they did with Michael, and yet the love for the game, the competitiveness, the passion, the desire to put on a show for the fans — there's such great comparisons to be made there," Kerr said.

Steve Kerr says watching Steph Curry during the late stages of his career reminds him of Michael Jordan. NBAE via Getty Images

Kerr added, "You get to experience the thrill of seeing them do their thing night in and night out, and it never gets old."

Kerr has won nine NBA titles, five as a player and four as a coach. Jordan won six NBA crowns as a player.

#3022. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%
