#2956. Bank of England hikes interest rate for the first time since Iran war, warns inflation will near 5%

The Bank of England announced its first interest rate hike in over three years on Thursday, raising its key rate by 25 basis points to 4%.

The widely anticipated move marked the bank's first hike since Aug. 2023, and made it the last major central bank to embark on a tightening cycle since the U.S. and Israel went to war with Iran eight months ago.

The majority of the bank's Monetary Policy Committee voted for the quarter-point hike, with the motion passing by 8 votes to 1. The dissenting member voted to raise the bank's key interest rate by 50 basis points to 4.25%.

"The conflict in the Middle East is now in its ninth month," the MPC said in a statement Thursday. "Global energy prices have continued to rise from an already elevated level."

Policymakers said they now expected U.K. inflation to rise to "about 5%" by the middle of 2027.

"It is then projected to fall as the impact of rising energy prices on 12-month inflation rates fades, and still restrictive monetary policy bears down on inflation," they said.

Ahead of the decision being announced, markets had been pricing in more than a 99% chance of a rate hike, according to LSEG data. Traders were also pricing in a one-in-four chance of a further hike in December.

Bank of England Governor Andrew Bailey said in the November Monetary Policy Report that without "real progress towards settling the conflict in the Middle East... risks to energy prices, and so to inflation, are to the upside."

"Without news which softens the risks to inflationary pressures, I can see the argument for a further increase in rates and thereafter taking a more conditional approach, dependent on the evidence on energy prices and inflation dynamics," he said.

Data released earlier this month showed that the U.K.'s annual inflation rate jumped to 3.5% in September, driven by the surging cost of motor fuels.

As a net energy importer, the U.K. is particularly vulnerable to external energy shocks — such as the one caused this year by the Iran war.

The MPC's most hawkish member, Catherine L Mann, said she had voted for greater tightening than her peers because research pointed to "non-linear inflation risks."

"My recent votes to increase Bank Rate by 25 basis points represented a 'down payment' to lean against second-round effects, reinforce credibility, and uphold 'good policy'," she said in comments published in the MPC's policy statement.

"The economy has crossed the estimated statistical threshold into a high inflation-tight labour market regime where the asymmetric behavioural responses of households and firms support more robust second-round effects," Mann added. "A different research approach estimates that the economy may well have breached the policy credibility threshold, which worsens the policy trade-off... From a risk management and credibility perspective, a 50-basis point hike in Bank Rate is 'good policy'."

Yields on U.K. government bonds, known as gilts, had been rising before the policy announcement and continued to edge higher after the Bank of England's update. The yield on the benchmark 10-year gilt was last seen 2 basis points higher at 5.1546%, while the 2-year gilt yield rose by 4 basis points to trade at 4.782%.

The British pound, which hit a post-Brexit high on Wednesday, was flat against both the U.S. dollar and the euro on Thursday afternoon.

Scott Gardner, investment strategist at JPMorgan Personal Investing, said in a note on Thursday that the November hike was "hugely symbolic."

"This is a 180-degree turn from the start of the year when many believed interest rates would come down as UK inflation had been mostly tamed," he said.

"The path ahead remains uncertain with much depending not only on how oil trades but also on natural gas prices which have risen sharply over the past few months. Worryingly, there are tentative signs of second round inflation effects in the UK economy as we have seen an acceleration in goods and services inflation. The Bank will be wary of this but also conscious of being too aggressive with rate hikes which could stunt the positive UK economic growth we've seen of late."

#2953. Rheinmetall shares plunge 17% after outlook cut as military contracts stall

German defense giant Rheinmetall cut its full-year guidance late Wednesday, citing delays to military contracts and ammunition deliveries.

It marks the company's second outlook cut since August, when Germany scrapped plans to build warships in what would have been Rheinmetall's biggest order since World War II. The defense company now expects sales to rise to around 13.2 billion euros ($14.9 billion) in 2026, down from a prior outlook range of between 13.7 billion and 14.2 billion euros. It is expected to have an operating margin of around 18.5%, down from the previously expected 19%.

Rheinmetall said it revised its 2026 guidance due to delays in the German military's Arminius project, a major German military procurement program for Boxer armored vehicles, and setbacks in artillery ammunition acceptance testing.

The Arminius contract, now expected to be commissioned next year, involves vehicles manufactured by ARTEC, a joint venture between Rheinmetall and the Franco-German defense group KNDS.

Rheinmetall shares fell over 17% in early trading, bringing year-to-date losses to about 46%.

It follows an average annual gain of 119% over the prior four years as it became one of the main beneficiaries of ramped-up military spending by European governments amid Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022.

RBC analysts called the announcement "clearly negative," citing concerns over the Boxer program's timing, scope and impact on medium-term guidance. They warned the news could fuel broader investor concerns about traditional defense technology.

"We suspect bears will see this negatively as part of the ongoing 'new-tech vs old-tech' sector debate," they said.

Rheinmetall said delays to the Boxer contract would also push expected advance payments into 2027, affecting its previous cash conversion target of over 40%, although it still expects positive operating free cash flow this year.

The company also flagged delays in expected fourth-quarter revenue from new business activities, particularly its partnership with Dutch defense technology firm Destinus.

The guidance cut came as the German company posted improving results, with third-quarter sales increasing 32% year-on-year to 3.15 billion euros and operating profit jumping 62% to 553 million euros.

New order intake was 2.89 billion euros, bringing its backlog to 78 billion euros by the end of the third quarter.

Despite being overshadowed by the outlook cut, Citi analysts called the third-quarter print "a strong set of Q3 results."

#2947. Slow start dooms Cavs in 124-116 loss to the Thunder

A 17-point first-quarter deficit was too much to overcome as the Cleveland Cavaliers fell to the Oklahoma City Thunder 124 to 116, dropping to .500 with a record of 4-4.

The Cavs made a brief comeback at the start of the third quarter with an 18-3 run spanning the end of the first half and the start of the second, but could never break through. They made things interesting late with a flurry of threes, but it was too late. James Harden, who finished with 19 points, nine rebounds, and nine assists, had 10 points in the third quarter to help bring Cleveland within striking distance, but the Thunder seemingly made a play right when they needed it.

Shai Gilgeous-Alexander dominated with 42 points, four rebounds, and eight assists. Ajay Mitchell had 23 points and seven rebounds in his expanded role in the wake of key injuries to Chet Holmgren and Jalen Williams.

The Cavs were without Peyton Watson (hamstring) and Jaylon Tyson (ankle), which led to a surprise start for Tyrese Proctor. The former Duke Blue Devil contributed 11 points on 4-of-7 shooting, but limped off the court late in the fourth quarter with an ankle injury.

Donovan Mitchell tried to will the Cavs to a shocker, drilling three huge threes with just a few minutes remaining, but to no avail. Mitchell finished with 27 points and six assists, though he was on pace for another pedestrian offensive showing before catching fire in the fourth quarter.

Evan Mobley had 16 points, 10 rebounds, and three blocks, and early bright spot Mario Hezonja provided 12 points and eight rebounds off the bench. Facing a big deficit and no spark, head coach Kenny Atkinson went deep off the bench to former Cleveland State forward Tristan Enaruna in the second quarter. Some new blood helped, but the Cavs could not fully dig themselves out of the first-quarter hole.

Center Jarrett Allen suffered a knee injury in the first half and was quickly deemed doubtful to return. Allen ended up with just one rebound in seven minutes of action. Hezonja started the second half in Allen's absence.

The shorthanded Thunder were relentless, building a big lead and then holding the Cavs at arm's length the rest of the way. They had a very sloppy end to the second quarter that allowed Cleveland to crack the door open, and then came out of the half just as flat. The Cavs managed to get within seven, but never closer until late in the game.

If you only judged by the first 15 minutes of game time, the Cavs looked like a team ready to get on the flight home. But there were some good things late in the game that should give them some confidence heading into a Friday night NBA Cup showdown with LeBron James and the Philadelphia 76ers. Tip-off is at 7:30 at Rocket Arena.

#2946. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2944. Figma sinks after earnings beat as company prioritizes long-term growth with AI changes

Figma shares tumbled 7% in extended trading after the company reported third-quarter results and guidance that surpassed Wall Street's predictions.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

Figma's revenue grew about 43% year over year in the quarter, according to a statement. Net loss narrowed to $81.3 million, or 15 cents per share, compared with a loss of $1.10 billion, or $2.72 per share, a year earlier.

The company's 12% adjusted operating margin exceeded the 6.1% consensus among analysts polled by StreetAccount.

Executives lifted the full-year forecast. Figma now anticipates an 11% operating margin, up from 9% as of August, and $1.497 billion to $1.499 billion in revenue, compared with $1.463 billion to $1.467 billion before. Analysts surveyed by LSEG were looking for $1.471 billion in 2026 revenue.

The company sees fourth-quarter revenue growth slowing to about 33%. Analysts polled by LSEG had predicted 28% growth.

Figma's announcement comes a day after highly valued design software startup Canva said it would cut 22% of its employees.

Shares have come down about 41% so far in 2026, while the S&P 500 index has gained 12%. Earlier this year, investors became concerned that artificial intelligence models could pose a competitive risk to Figma. Across much of software, AI panic has largely subsided as incumbents have posted persistent growth.

Figma wants to benefit from the rising adoption of AI to build websites and apps. During the quarter, the company started giving more AI credits to clients while maintaining prices.

"What that means is you're potentially trading off near-term revenue, investing in your customers, and then allowing yourself to then grow durably over the long term," Figma's finance chief, Praveer Melwani, told CNBC in an interview on Wednesday.

The number of weekly users working at organizations that spend over $10,000 on Figma who used AI credits increased by almost 50% during the quarter, Figma said in the statement. The share of work the company's own AI model performs doubled, and the rate of people undoing AI-generated work has fallen.

"If there's certain actions that we can power for our customers at lower latency, lower cost, or similar quality to third party, we're open to that as well," Melwani said.

Figma is increasing its focus on cybersecurity, said Dylan Field, Figma's co-founder and CEO. In recent months, AI agents have infiltrated systems at Hugging Face and other companies.

Clients aren't asking Figma to become a security vendor, but the company must scrutinize "the security of all of our vendors right now," Field said.

#2937. Rapid Recap: Celtics 109, Bucks 102

Despite being down by 21 at halftime, the Milwaukee Bucks were able to battle back and outscore the Boston Celtics in the second half, 61-47, but ultimately fell short and could not secure their first road win of the year, losing 109-102. Tyler Herro led all Bucks scorers with 25 points, while Paul George led the Cs with 25 as well.

The game began with the Bucks scoring just five points—all from Jaime Jaquez Jr.—in the first four minutes while turning it over three times. The most optimistic fans (me) could watch and think, "Once they settle in, these shots will fall," but nothing was further from the truth. After Myles Turner hit his first three to make it 12-8, the Bucks didn't score for almost six minutes as the Celtics went on a 15-0 run; Nate Ament ended the drought with a corner three. I mean, Brayden Burries secured a couple of nice offensive rebounds, but it was otherwise a quarter to forget. The visitors looked out of sync and lacked any identity, down 28-14 after one.

The second quarter started at a much better pace, with Milwaukee going mostly blow for blow to keep the deficit at 14 through the first half of the quarter. Once Boston's starting lineup was back on the floor, though, they took the pace over again, and the Bucks looked handcuffed. By the end of the second quarter, the Bucks had committed 10 turnovers for 12 Celtics points, and the Bucks were lucky it wasn't more. Milwaukee was also totally ineffective in transition, with a points-per-possession of -7.4; ironically, their transition play has been one of their few strengths this year, but over two quarters, they couldn't find any rhythm there. Milwaukee also didn't attack the rim, with just five total shot attempts at the rim, converting only one. The lone silver lining in the first half was Myles Turner, who was really having a game: nine points, five rebounds, two assists, two steals, and a block. Still, the Bucks were down 62-41 at the half.

The second half started with a much faster pace, with a 12-5 run pulling the Bucks back within 14 and forcing Boston to take a timeout. Most of the scoring and pace came from Jaquez, who stormed out of the locker room and put up eight points in the first few minutes. The pace continued, led mostly by Herro, and at one point the Bucks made eight straight buckets from the field, including a sick Burries dunk that forced another Boston timeout with the visitors down nine, 74-65. Milwaukee looked like a totally refreshed team, crashing the boards and moving the ball exceptionally well, all while finally protecting the ball (only one turnover for the quarter). Milwaukee shot a white-hot 14/21 in the third, down 87-79 entering the final frame.

The Bucks kept fighting to start the fourth, largely thanks to Nate Ament. Ament looked impressive, playing incredibly effectively off-ball and helping his teammates find opportunities to score, while also hitting some big-time shots himself; he hit a triple from the top of the key and then a layup to tie it up at 91. Burries subbed in at the six-minute mark and helped recapture the lead with a sweet 14-foot floater as the game turned into a sludge fest, with the lead changing on what felt like every possession. Make no mistake, the Bucks had multiple chances to take the game, but could not execute when it mattered, unlike the Celtics. Boston was just way better at making the crucial play, converting just enough of their opportunities to stave off the Bucks' comeback.

Four: the number of turnovers the Bucks committed in the whole second half. Milwaukee looked like a different team, and their ability to move the ball efficiently in the second half was the biggest reason.

I've seen enough: Start Nate Ament at the four. I made a similar call in the preseason after Dieng had a solid outing, so this is becoming something of a habit for me, but Ament played with infectious energy tonight that helped keep the Bucks in it despite being down 21 at halftime. On the flip side: Kyle Kuzma is arguably more responsible for the last two losses than anyone else. Low effort, high turnovers, and not good enough at anything to justify starting him or closing games. Let Ament start and let him get battle-tested now.

#2933. Democrats take Congress in a blue wave. Here's what it means for your money

With Democrats flipping control of Congress in the midterm election, the shift in power on Capitol Hill could reshape household budgets — from student loan bills and health insurance premiums to housing costs and taxes.

Experts say results show that rising costs were top of mind for voters.

"I see this election as a repudiation of the anti-affordability agenda from the Trump administration," said Daniel Bunn, president and CEO of the Tax Foundation, a nonpartisan, nonprofit research organization.

"Whether that's the conflict in the Middle East or the tariffs, just top to bottom, it's clear to me that was a real concern of the electorate," he said.

White House spokesman Matthew Middleton said in an emailed statement that the Trump administration has delivered "tangible wins to make life more affordable for American families." Officials "will continue to put hardworking American's first with or without support from Democrats," he wrote.

Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and Senate, which could make it easier for the party to advance legislation. They can also use hearings and investigations to press federal agencies and companies to answer for policies and practices that impact consumers, and exert leverage over confirmation of President Donald Trump's nominees.

But turning campaign promises into changes people feel in their finances won't be easy. Trump can veto bills a Democrat-controlled Congress passes, limiting how much of their agenda could become law. Based on projections, Democrats will lack the numbers needed to override a presidential veto.

Any changes will also take time: Newly elected members of Congress won't take office until January.

Here are eight ways Democrats’ new congressional majority could affect your money.

"Not surprisingly, the Dems are going to be keyed in on affordability with respect to healthcare," said Zach Gaumer, vice president of health policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonpartisan think tank.

OBBBA made Trump's 2017 tax cuts permanent and added temporary tax breaks. These included new deductions for tip income, overtime earnings, seniors and auto loan interest, which will expire after 2028 without an extension.

Democrats may support extending these provisions. But as the Democrat-controlled Congress maps out its agenda, lawmakers will have to address the federal budget deficit. For fiscal year 2026, the budget deficit ballooned to nearly $2 trillion, the Treasury announced in October.

Newly elected Democratic senators James Talarico of Texas, Troy Jackson of Maine and Annie Andrews of South Carolina all campaigned on expanding the child tax credit. Earlier this year, Senate Democrats also floated ideas to expand the standard deduction and create income tax exemptions for lower-earning Americans — which are costly proposals, according to Bunn from the Tax Foundation.

"I've called these termites in the tax code," he said. "You cannot consistently narrow your base, especially for the income tax, and expect your spending priorities to be well-funded."

With Democrats in control of Congress, they are likely to hold hearings and subpoena Trump officials over the administration's student loan policies, said higher education expert Mark Kantrowitz.

One priority may be pressing the Education Department to fix the popular Public Service Loan Forgiveness program, which cancels the remaining federal student debt of eligible government and nonprofit workers after a decade. Over the summer, Trump officials reassessed repayment timelines, delaying some borrowers' forgiveness date by years.

Broad student loan forgiveness is unlikely: Trump could veto such a bill, and the Supreme Court blocked the Biden administration's plan. More attainable changes could include restoring tax-free treatment of forgiven student debt, raising the new borrowing caps and expanding repayment options — measures that may attract bipartisan support, Kantrowitz said.

Trump has also said he may support legislation capping federal student loan interest rates, a proposal that could give Democrats common ground in their push to ease borrowers’ costs.

Housing affordability was a key election issue, and Democrats will likely explore ways to deliver relief, such as introducing measures to help first-time homebuyers, according to an Oct. 30 analysis by Desjardins, a Canadian financial services company.

However, lawmakers generally have little direct impact on home prices, which are based on market conditions. Mortgage rates, too, are influenced by forces generally beyond congressional control. The yield on the 10-year Treasury, which remains higher than it was before the Iran war — due at least partly to concerns about inflation eroding the value of those bonds — influences mortgage rates.

"Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run," plus more housing supply and a "more dynamic labor market in the long run," said Jake Krimmel, senior economist at Realtor.com.

The bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which automatically became law July 11 without the president's signature due to a constitutional provision, is "a model for what can pass in a divided Washington," Krimmel said. It includes measures to encourage housing construction, reduce regulatory barriers, expand financing options and limit purchases of single-family homes by large institutional investors.

The incoming class of senators will have to address Social Security insolvency.

The program's trustees project that the trust fund Social Security relies on to help pay retirement benefits, formally known as the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund, may be depleted in the fourth quarter of 2032. Without action from Congress before then, benefits could face an across-the-board cut of 22%.

A number of Democratic lawmakers and some Republicans have called for increased payroll taxes on high earners to help shore up Social Security.

A potential SECURE 3.0 bill — a follow-up to bipartisan retirement-savings laws passed in 2019 and 2022 — could help expand workers' access to workplace retirement plans like 401(k)s, for example, among a roster of other reforms, experts said.

"Trump promised to cap credit card rates at 10%," said Aaron Klein, a senior fellow in economic studies at the Brookings Institution, a think tank. "A Democratic Congress might be willing to do that."

Any such cap is likely to be closer to 36% than to the 10% cap Trump has sought, Jaret Seiberg, a financial services policy analyst at TD Cowen, an investment bank, wrote in a research note Wednesday. Lawmakers in the current Congress have proposed legislation with these thresholds.

A cap would likely be welcome news for low- and middle-income Americans, Klein said. They are more likely than wealthy households to use debt to finance their lives and to carry a credit card balance — and therefore owe interest on their charges, he said.

Cardholders might see other impacts of such a policy, such as lower credit limits and a clawback of certain card rewards, he said.

Democrats' takeover of Congress could stall efforts to put crypto-friendly regulations into federal law, but everyday investors shouldn't expect much to change immediately.

"Platforms will keep offering the products they offer today, and they still have friendly regulators at the SEC and CFTC who are approving an ever-wider range of crypto products," said Lee Reiners, a cryptocurrency regulation expert and lecturing fellow of economics at Duke University.

The election results could threaten the Clarity Act, which would give the Commodity Futures Trading Commission greater authority over cryptocurrency trading while reducing the Securities and Exchange Commission's role. The bill passed the House in July 2025 by a 294-134 vote, but failed to advance in the Senate in September on a 49-50 procedural vote. "CLARITY is dead," said Reiners, adding that he sees little chance of the bill passing during the lame-duck session before Democrats take control in January.

Any future legislation aimed at creating a regulatory framework for digital assets would likely need to address Democrats' concerns about investor protections and conflicts of interest involving Trump's crypto businesses, said Martin Mulyadi, an accounting professor at Shenandoah University.

The SEC and CFTC can also continue developing crypto-friendly rules without Congress. But those rules are easier for a future administration to reverse than legislation, said Marcel Miu, a certified financial planner and founder of Simplify Wealth Planning based in Austin, Texas. "I'd tell investors to size their crypto investments as if the rules could change repeatedly over the coming years," Miu said.

The Iran war has led oil prices — and those of gasoline, jet fuel, heating oil, diesel and other fuels refined from oil — to surge. That has trickled into higher prices across the U.S. economy, experts said.

Oil prices fell on Wednesday on hopes that the election outcome will push Trump to make a deal to end the war.

"Ending the war in Iran would be the biggest policy lever to reducing inflation," the Brookings Institution's Klein said.

A Democrat-led Congress could try to limit Trump's ability to continue the war, perhaps by cutting funding.

"Congress' biggest power is the power of the purse," Klein said. "Wars are expensive."

— CNBC's Lorie Konish and Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting.

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2926. Nets find way to finish this time for payback win over Hornets

Monday in the Garden, the Nets let a sorry fourth quarter spoil three solid ones.

But two nights later, they closed the deal.

This time the Nets held their poise and held on for a comfortable 114-104 victory over visiting Charlotte.

Michael Porter Jr. (left) and Julius Randle high-five during the Nets' 114-104 win over the Hornets on Nov. 4, 2026 at Barclays Center. NBAE via Getty Images

It was payback for a humbling season-opening loss against these same Hornets. And after a win in Indiana — and taking a lead into the fourth quarter on the road at the reigning NBA champion Knicks — there are signs of the Nets making strides.

"We were in the same position a couple nights ago in New York and fell apart in the fourth," Michael Porter Jr. said. "So, that's what good teams do, growing teams do, is they learn from their failure and try to not do that same thing again. So I was proud of everybody."

Porter had 26 points and 10 rebounds, taking over late. Julius Randle scored 22 while they got solid play from the young point guard tandem of starter Nolan Traore (16 points, three steals, plus-13) and rookie Mikel Brown Jr. (career-high 18 points, four assists).

But most auspicious was the Nets' fourth quarter offense. After imploding two nights earlier — when they coughed up a 25-5 run to open the final period, and shot 3-of-16 with seven turnovers — this time they looked like a cohesive unit.

The Nets (2-6) outscored the Hornets 34-24 in the fourth, shooting 51.2 percent and 6-of-11 from deep. And Porter — who had been scoreless in the fourth against the Knicks — had 13 points on 4-of-4 from behind the arc.

"Fourth quarter is winning time," Porter said. "You can play a good three quarters of basketball; the fourth quarter's a completely different game. You have to be more intentional with your cuts, your setups, the speed in which you play, your defensive intensity.

Mikel Brown Jr. celebrates after hitting a 3-pointer during the Nets' win over the Hornets. Corey Sipkin for the NY POST

"In the fourth quarter, you've got to be better at communicating; you've got to be more disciplined; be more alert. Offensively, we were a lot more connected, and we had a lot of intention and speed with how we played. That's what it takes in the fourth quarter in a close game. So if we can build off of that, that'll be great."

The Nets' defense was stout from the start, forcing six straight misses and a turnover to open the game.

A Randle 3-pointer gave the Nets a 40-37 edge with 5:42 left in the half.

They surrendered it to an extended 20-7 Hornets run, falling behind by 10 on a Grayson Allen 3-pointer with a minute left in the first half.

The Nets were still down 59-50 a minute into the second.

But they promptly reeled off 11 unanswered to take the momentum and the lead.

Danny Wolf (right) celebrates with Terance Mann during the second half of the Nets' win over the Hornets. Getty Images

Jordi Fernández switched up his rotation, partly because of need. With Noah Clowney forced out by right knee soreness, Fernández went to second-year forward Danny Wolf, and also replaced 6-8, 235 pound Chaney Johnson with guard Terance Mann. The extra ballhandling paid dividends.

Wolf (12 points, plus-17) capped the run on a 3-pointer off a Randle feed, giving the Nets a 61-59 edge.

Even after they coughed it up to fall behind 78-72, they seized it back with a 17-5 run that spanned the third and fourth quarters. Randle found Brown for a 3 that put the Nets ahead 89-83.

"We just came together in the fourth quarter, and we understood the assignment," Brown said. "This has been the theme of we haven't been finishing games and now we finished it."

This time — unlike two nights earlier in the Garden — they held their poise. And held on for the victory.

"It shows that we definitely went back to the film. … [The coaches] told us how to win a game and we went out there and executed it," Mann said. "This game could've very likely went how the game in the Garden went. We were in the same situation and we flipped the script."

#2925. OpenAI pledges to halt NDAs in new data center policy proposal after election rebuke

OpenAI reaffirmed its promise to pay the full cost of powering data centers as part of a series of commitments the company published Thursday, two days after voters at the polls roundly rejected the rapid expansion of AI.

The artificial intelligence company said in its commitments that for data centers it owns and operates, it will solicit community input, publish construction plans and stay away from using non-disclosure agreements with public officials. Additionally, in a policy blueprint, OpenAI encouraged policymakers to enact similar commitments as laws.

"What we're doing with this blueprint is really laying out the best lessons that we have learned and committing to applying them as they go forward," OpenAI Chief Global Affairs Officer Chris Lehane told reporters during a Wednesday briefing. "This will help ensure that there is a very high floor in terms of how people move forward."

Other tech companies, including Meta, Microsoft, Google and Anthropic, have outlined several similar commitments.

On Tuesday, Americans issued a stinging rebuke of data centers at the polls. Out of 41 local ballot measures across 10 states, voters backed restrictions or rejected pro-development measures for all but one, according to an analysis by Ballotpedia and additional results gathered by CNBC on Wednesday.

Opposition to AI data centers mounted swiftly over the summer across both sides of the political aisle. Consumers grappling with inflation, rising grocery prices and stubbornly high prices at the pump grew worried that the facilities could exacerbate environmental harms, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss. For many, they became a physical manifestation of the widespread antipathy and anxiety around AI.

AI companies like OpenAI depend on AI data centers to serve customers and develop new models, and they're seeking to build out as rapidly as possible to meet demand. In February, OpenAI told investors that it's targeting $600 billion in total compute spend by 2030, and the company has since announced massive data center projects in Georgia and Ohio.

OpenAI has repeatedly pledged to protect ratepayers and invest in local communities this year, and Lehane said the company's latest commitments build on many of its previous initiatives. He said the policy blueprint has been in the works since August, and that he met with dozens of lawmakers and candidates since then.

"Having these conversations now actually helps put more energy into the momentum, so that when people come back in late January, new Congress is in in session, a lot of the work is being done in advance," Lehane said.

#2921. What the Democratic midterms sweep changes for U.S.-China relations

BEIJING — Democrats taking power from Republicans in Congress raises risks for U.S.-China tensions around Taiwan, while hardening tech competition, analysts said.

The Democrats swept this week's midterm elections, taking majorities in the House and Senate and flipping governors' offices in places like Texas, Nevada and Iowa. Most of the new election winners, however, won't begin their terms until January.

Before then, U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping will likely maintain their fragile truce by meeting twice: in Shenzhen this month, and in Miami, Florida, next month.

"As a lame duck who is increasingly focused on his personal legacy rather than the Republican Party's success, Trump may resist increased pressure for confrontation with China and continue his pursuit of 'strategic stability,'" Gabriel Wildau, managing director at Teneo, said in an email.

High on the Democrats' agenda is removing Trump's tariffs, as part of a broader focus on addressing domestic inflation, CNBC has learned.

"On balance as a result of the midterms the Democrats may nudge the U.S.'s China policy in a more hawkish direction through hearings and legislation, particularly on economic security issues and Taiwan," said Scott Kennedy, senior advisor and trustee chair in Chinese business and economics at the Center for Strategic and International Studies.

But any efforts will "be constrained by the executive branch's wide range of latitude and China's growing power and leverage, which can't be easily undone," he said.

Beijing has hosted a flurry of global leaders this year, while major economies have struggled to slow a surge of imports from China. Chinese dominance in rare earths has given Beijing leverage in trade talks with the U.S. and the EU.

"Taiwan remains the most dangerous issue in U.S.-China relations and, in my view, the area where a Democratic Congress is most likely to challenge Beijing," wrote George Chen, partner and co-chair of digital practice at consultancy The Asia Group.

"Congress has repeatedly shown a willingness to pursue Taiwan initiatives even when administrations are more cautious," he said, noting Trump's balancing act will likely become more difficult.

Nancy Pelosi's visit to Taiwan while House Speaker in 2022 angered Beijing, which considers the democratically self-governed island part of its territory. The U.S. maintains unofficial relations with Taiwan under a "One China" policy.

Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.), who is poised to become the first Black Speaker of the House in U.S. history, met in 2023 in New York with Tsai Ing-wen, who was then president of Taiwan.

During Trump's trip to Beijing in May, Xi warned that mishandling Taiwan would put U.S.-China relations in "great jeopardy."

Maintaining stability over Taiwan has grown more critical in the artificial intelligence race as the island is home to semiconductor giant TSMC, which manufactures many of Nvidia's chips.

Over the last several years, the U.S. government has tightened restrictions on the ability of Chinese companies to buy advanced chips from Nvidia, spurring Beijing's efforts to develop its own version of the technology.

U.S. stocks surged Wednesday after Trump accepted the Democrats' election wins, and oil prices fell following indications Washington would do more to end the Iran war. Apple and most "Magnificent Seven" tech stocks surged, despite declines in Nvidia.

But there's an "emergence of a broader consensus in Washington that America's technological lead should not be used to strengthen China's military or strategic capabilities," Chen said, noting a Democratic Congress may be motivated to be more aggressive.

"As a result, Beijing may discover that technology becomes a growing source of friction even if Trump and Xi continue to maintain relatively cordial summit diplomacy," he said.

The tech outlook ultimately depends on bigger questions about AI.

Chinese tech stocks climbed Thursday, extending a recent rebound from a sharp sell-off last month.

Beijing's latest regulatory intervention has helped support the market, said WisdomTree's Liqian Ren. But she said Chinese tech stock performance is ultimately tied more to global investor sentiment on AI.

"There's a lot of uncertainty on how much AI can deliver for U.S., or for China," she said.

—CNBC's Justin Papp and Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#2916. LIV Golf faces liquidation after BC Partners scraps rescue plan

LIV Golf could be headed for liquidation after BC Partners Credit reportedly dropped plans to finance a relaunch of the breakaway league.

The investment firm had pledged up to $300 million for a revamped version of LIV, known as LIV 2.0, but withdrew the plan after LIV Golf failed to secure enough player commitments, according to the Associated Press.

LIV is scheduled to appear in U.S. bankruptcy court Thursday, with the hearing now expected to focus on liquidation of its assets. The league's ultimate fate remains unclear.

LIV Golf and BC Partners did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

LIV filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection in September after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund said it would stop funding the league after the 2026 season.

PIF had invested more than $5 billion since LIV launched in 2022, according to its Chapter 11 filing.

BC Partners' proposed relaunch would have made players majority owners of the reorganized league and featured a scaled-back schedule.

But LIV has lost several prominent players, including Jon Rahm and Sergio Garcia, as it sought commitments for the new venture.

Rahm said Tuesday that uncertainty over the league's future and proposed changes to its format influenced his decision to leave.

"There were too many unknowns at the time, and there's still quite a few," he told reporters ahead of the Abu Dhabi Championship. Rahm added that the revamped competition appeared to be moving away from the team format that initially attracted him.

Garcia, who secured a release from his LIV contract in October, has outlined plans to return to the DP World Tour and explore a possible return to the PGA Tour.

Only four players had publicly committed to LIV 2.0: Richard Bland, Cameron Tringale, Brendan Steele and Ben Campbell, according to Golf Digest.

LIV launched in 2022 as a challenger to the PGA Tour, recruiting prominent players including Phil Mickelson, Dustin Johnson and Bryson DeChambeau with lucrative contracts.

Its arrival split men's professional golf, with the PGA Tour suspending members who competed in LIV events. The disputes later spilled into the courts, with players challenging suspensions and the rival organizations pursuing competing legal claims.

The PGA Tour denied the allegations, while LIV dismissed the counterclaims.

A June 2023 framework agreement between the PGA Tour, DP World Tour and PIF ended their litigation and proposed combining their commercial businesses, although the deal was never completed.

NEW YORK (AP) — Michael Porter Jr. had 26 points and 10 rebounds and Julius Randle added 22 points as the Brooklyn Nets won their second game of the season, 114-104, over the Charlotte Hornets on Wednesday night.

#2913. Karl and Laimer on target as Bayern Munich cruise past Atlético Madrid

Looking to get back to winning ways in Europe after drawing 1-1 with Stuttgart last time out, Atlético Madrid made a lively start as Bayern visited the Metropolitano, with talisman Julian Alvarez curling an effort narrowly wide of Manuel Neuer's far post inside three minutes.

Bayern then quickly took control, but despite enjoying over 70 per cent of the ball in the opening 20 minutes, they failed to register a shot, while Atléti midfielder Pablo Barrios saw a deflected effort fly just over.

Lennart Karl threatened midway through the half, firing over from the edge of the box, while Kane put an effort just wide following a delivery from Konrad Laimer.

On the half-hour mark, the hosts should have taken the lead, with Alexander Sørloth handed a chance he will not want to see again, with the Norwegian unable to sort his feet out following a ball to the back stick from Marc Pubill.

And after both sides failed to manage a shot on target before the break, Joshua Kimmich finally tested Jan Oblak shortly after the restart, before Karl broke the deadlock in the 54th minute.

Robin Le Normand appeared to have the ball under control near the penalty spot after blocking an effort from Tom Bischof, but the youngster then stole possession from behind the Spanish defender and fired the visitors into the lead.

Koke, Ademola Lookman and Lee Kang-in would come on for the hosts five minutes later, and Los Colchoneros began to find their feet after the changes, with Marcos Llorente forcing Neuer into his first save of the evening in the 67th minute.

Goalscorer Karl was replaced by Nathaniel Brown shortly afterwards, before Kane went close to doubling Bayern's advantage, only for the flag to be raised after the England captain lifted an effort narrowly over after rounding the onrushing Oblak.

Bayern would double their lead in the 74th minute, when Laimer hammered in off the crossbar from a tight angle following a beautifully clipped ball from Kane.

In the ascendancy and carrying a two-goal lead, the record German champions were well on track to pick up their first Champions League win since the opening week triumph against Bodø/Glimt.

Bayern did think they had a third in the 82nd minute, when Dayot Upamecano headed home from a corner, before the effort was ruled out for a foul, and left-back Brown then had a chance to extend the lead, only to be denied by Oblak on the angle.

Seeing out the win, Bayern take three points back to Munich with them and turn their European form back around after successive draws, while attention now turns to Saturday's trip to Mainz.

#2912. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2910. What Wall Street billionaires are saying about Democrats' midterm sweep

America's billionaires woke up Wednesday to a political reckoning — and a Wall Street rally.

As Democrats celebrated a sweeping midterm victory, some of the country's wealthiest donors traded barbs over record election spending. Others warned the results could threaten the artificial intelligence boom.

Wall Street, however, cheered the outcome. The Dow Jones Industrial Average soared 1,569 points, or 3%, while the S&P 500 gained 1.6% and the Nasdaq Composite rose 1.4%, as investors welcomed political gridlock, reduced uncertainty and President Donald Trump's willingness to accept the results.

Virgin Group founder Richard Branson took aim at the billions spent influencing the election.

"I have felt embarrassed by the billions that tech entrepreneurs and hedge fund managers have spent," Branson wrote Wednesday on LinkedIn, calling for campaign finance reform. Branson did not give donations that met the reporting threshold in the 2026 election cycle.

His comments came as Elon Musk, the election's largest individual donor, watched his preferred Republicans lose the Texas Senate and governor races despite pouring $224 million into the state — nearly half his record $474 million in political giving nationwide, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records.

Early Wednesday, the Tesla and SpaceX CEO suggested on X that data centers in space were looking increasingly appealing before joking that his "election-interfering space lasers" had failed.

LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, another major political donor, took a sharper tone.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote late Tuesday on X, accusing Musk of spending "outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power" while defending his own contributions to Democrats.

Other Democratic megadonors celebrated. "The world changed yesterday," Alex Soros wrote Wednesday on X, hailing the results as a generational victory after his family contributed roughly $103 million to Democratic political efforts.

But beyond the political sparring, some of the country's richest investors were focused on a potentially bigger consequence of the Democratic sweep: the future of the AI boom.

Even before polls closed Tuesday, Pershing Square founder Bill Ackman warned on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that slowing AI development could jeopardize America's technological edge. "Practically speaking, we can't slow down," he said.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, billionaire investor Chamath Palihapitiya sounded an even starker warning.

Palihapitiya initially questioned whether the early results would hold, but warned that if they did, the implications for the AI trade could be severe.

"And the only way to sink the AI trade is to sink the data centers," Palihapitiya said during an election night livestream of the "All-In" podcast.

Texas offered an early test of those fears. Democratic Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa, who has proposed a moratorium on new data center construction until stronger safeguards are established, defeated three-term Republican incumbent Greg Abbott.

During the same livestream, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner warned the Democratic wave could mean at least 10 to 15 gigawatts of expected AI data center capacity would not come online in 2027. Wall Street analyst Dan Ives estimated Wednesday that 10% of planned U.S. data centers would no longer go ahead.

Advanced Micro Devices CEO Lisa Su acknowledged the growing resistance to AI's expansion, urging technology companies to demonstrate the benefits of data centers to local communities.

"At the end of the day, what we need to do is ensure that everybody wins from AI," Su told CNBC on Wednesday.

Yet Wall Street may have found opportunity in the same slowdown that worried AI's biggest backers.

Fewer new data centers could increase the value of existing facilities while easing the enormous spending burden on technology giants. Big Tech stocks surged Wednesday, adding more than $750 billion in market value at one point, even as smaller, debt-dependent data center operators tumbled.

#2909. Nintendo Switch 2 sales plunge 41% as price hikes and memory crunch bite

Sales of Nintendo's flagship Switch 2 plunged in its fiscal second quarter after the Japanese gaming giant rolled out price hikes amid soaring memory costs.

Nintendo said it sold 2.67 million units of the Switch 2 in the three months ended Sept. 30, down 41% from the same period last year. That was also lower than the 3.82 million units sold in the prior quarter.

#2908. World's largest olive oil company plummets 20% after rivals agree to takeover deal

Shares of Deoleo, the world's largest olive oil company, plummeted Thursday after two rivals announced plans to acquire the company, a deal that would further consolidate Spain's dominance in the sector.

Spanish olive oil firms Dcoop and Acesur will each hold a 50% stake in the company, resulting in a new olive oil giant in the region.

The deal is intended to boost olive oil consumption worldwide and support farmers in creating value through the supply chain, Dcoop said in an announcement.

Deoleo was last trading down 19.9%. However the stock is up around 55% since the beginning of the year as it's benefited from a moderation in olive oil prices that has helped lift U.S. sales.

CNBC reached out to Deoleo for comment and is awaiting a response.

The news comes after an intensifying takeover battle between Dcoop and multinational firms including those from Italy, France, and Australia. The stock gained over 20% on Aug. 19 when news of the takeover battle emerged. Dcoop reportedly previously offered 470 million euros ($545 million) for Deoleo.

Spain is one of the world's leading olive oil producers and is often used as a global reference for prices.

Prices for the commodity have been volatile over the past few years as supplies swung dramatically one season to the next, with climate change, water scarcity, and pest and disease playing a role.

Deoleo CEO Cristóbal Valdés told CNBC in July that the period between 2022 and 2024 was one of the most challenging periods for the sector, but that prices are now stabilizing due to favourable rain trends.

"The highly complex market cycle experienced between 2022 and 2024, which had a severe yet temporary impact on the industry, is now definitively behind us," Valdés said at the time.

#2907. Banks are turning cautious on lending, according to the Fed. What it means for the economy

U.S. banks are starting to tighten their lending standards amid growing concern over the underlying health of the economy, according to a new Federal Reserve survey.

The Fed's latest Senior Loan Officer Opinion Survey on Bank Lending Practices for October, published Nov. 2, showed an uneven credit backdrop in the third quarter, with smaller businesses, riskier households and commercial property in particular facing a lending squeeze.

The latest quarterly SLOOS study for October — which pulled together responses from 61 domestic banks and 18 U.S. branches of foreign lenders — covered changes in lending standards, terms and demand during the third quarter.

The survey tracks banks' reported lending standards, terms and demand, not actual loan volumes, offering a snapshot of evolving credit conditions and sentiment.

Commercial and industrial lending to large and middle-market firms was broadly unchanged from the previous survey — but banks tightened lending to smaller businesses.

"Banks reported tightening standards for premiums charged on riskier loans for firms of all sizes," the report noted. Lenders pointed to a less favorable or more uncertain economic outlook, and a worsening of industry-specific problems as the key reason for the shift.

Commercial real estate is also emerging as a pressure point, with banks reporting tightened standards across the board on construction and land development, nonfarm nonresidential properties, and multifamily properties, and weakening demand for loans in all CRE categories.

U.S. banks are also tightening credit standards for nonbank financial firms such as private equity funds and business and mortgage credit intermediaries. The pullback comes amid growing demand for liquidity from private equity funds and business credit intermediaries, with lenders again highlighting prevailing economic uncertainty and concerns over the deteriorating financial strength among NDFI borrowers.

Meanwhile, household credit sentiment appears mixed.

While banks have left mortgage standards broadly unchanged, there has been some easing for jumbo mortgages — but tighter rules for subprime loans — as banks reported weaker demand, on balance, across all residential real estate loan categories.

"Banks reported being more likely to approve credit card applications to super-prime and prime borrowers and less likely to approve applications to subprime borrowers, with small banks driving the trend for subprime borrowers," the report added.

The squeeze comes as borrowing costs moved sharply higher during the third quarter. The 10-year Treasury note yield — the main benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — hit an intraday high of 5.349% on Oct. 5, its highest level since April 2002, amid concerns over rising U.S. debt.

Tiffany Wilding, managing director and economist at PIMCO, said small businesses, housing and debt-laden firms face tighter credit and higher borrowing costs as the AI investment boom squeezes other parts of the U.S. economy.

"Higher government bond yields have catalyzed a broader repricing of credit risk, especially for lower-quality borrowers," Wilding said in a note Oct. 28.

Signals of credit stress among riskier households chime with a recent CNBC and SurveyMonkey poll, which found that one in five Americans who rent or have a mortgage spend at least half of their pretax income on housing, while 49% spend between 25% and 49%. Lender guidance recommends consumers spend 28% to 30% of their income on housing.

A separate Federal Reserve report indicated that U.S. families' ability to address their debts has worsened over the past three years. The Fed's latest Survey of Consumer Finances, released Oct. 9, found that the percentage of families behind on loan payments at the end of 2025 reached almost 20%, up from about 12% in the previous survey in 2022.

#2904. Voters rejected AI data centers over resource concerns. Here's why measuring water usage is hard

Voters made it clear this week that they want to see limits put on data centers for artificial intelligence, passing 40 of 41 proposed ballot measures that would place restrictions on some of the more than 2,000 new facilities planned for communities around the nation.

Water and power top the list of ratepayers' concerns. But calculating how much each data center draws from local resources is an elusive task, with operators typically reporting usage figures companywide instead of site by site.

Giant 1-gigawatt AI data centers from the likes of Meta, Google, Amazon and SpaceX can consume up to 10 million gallons of water per day, according to Shaolei Ren, a data center researcher and professor at University of California, Riverside.

The exact number varies greatly depending on what type of hardware is inside a data center, where it's built, and what method is used to cool the servers and the building as a whole.

The complex ways data centers use water have left room for misinformation from both the builders and those fighting against them. CNBC talked to experts to get a clearer picture.

Data centers need serious internal cooling methods to keep chips and servers from overheating.

Legacy data centers, filled with lower-power servers used for things like networking or storage, are often air-cooled. Large fans move hot air away from the servers and outside the building.

But modern AI data centers are filled with high-power, denser graphics processing units, or GPUs, which Ren said can run so hot that the servers can "boil your eggs."

Water is about 3,000 times more efficient for cooling down these GPUs, according to Equinix sustainability head Christopher Wellise.

GPUs "run at such a high wattage, a high temperature, you would need nearly hurricane-force winds to actually cool them down if you were just using air," Wellise told CNBC in an interview.

That's why the latest rack-scale AI systems, Nvidia's Vera Rubin and Advanced Micro Devices' Helios, rely on liquid cooling. Most large AI data centers use a closed-loop liquid cooling system that uses very little water on a daily basis.

At an Equinix data center in San Jose, California, CNBC got an up-close look at the closed-loop liquid cooling system used to keep temperatures down in a row of eight Nvidia Grace Blackwell racks owned by AI marketing startup Alembic.

Equinix builds colocation data centers, where companies like Alembic rent space to run their own server racks, while Equinix supplies the power, network connections, interconnectivity and cooling.

Like many data center operators, Equinix doesn't disclose water consumption at individual sites. In 2025, Equinix operations as a whole consumed 1.2 billion gallons of water, up 10% from a year prior.

Despite closed-loop internal cooling like what Equinix uses in San Jose, total water consumption is far more dependent on an additional cooling process that happens outside the data center.

"How do we reject the heat from the closed loop to the ambient environment?" Ren said. "That's where the water usage comes in."

In Lebanon, Indiana, for example, Meta is building a 1GW data center that it says uses a "closed-loop, liquid-cooled system that recirculates the same water and will use zero water for a majority of the year."

Water allocation for the site, however, could ramp up to 8 million gallons per day by 2031, according to the company's law firm. Meta says that figure represents peak usage on the hottest days. "We anticipate rarely reaching this maximum usage level, currently projected as only a few days per year," Meta told CNBC in a statement.

That's because the hottest days of the year will require more water for that external cooling process, removing the heat from that separate internal closed-loop system.

There are two primary methods for this external cooling process at a data center: dry cooling and evaporative cooling. While dry cooling uses far less water, it can require far more power, especially in hotter climates where many of these AI data centers are being built.

"There's no sort of silver bullet," Wellise said. "At some point you need to eject that heat out to the atmosphere. And that's where those trade-offs occur."

Dry cooling relies on fans to blow air across heat exchangers, using the ambient air to cool it down. Those fans are less effective when the outside temperature is high, so data centers turn on refrigeration units to cool down the air first.

"That's a very power-intensive process, and that's exactly why we have power shortages in the summer," Ren said. "Because everybody else turns on their AC."

Evaporative cooling, meanwhile, uses very little power. The hot water circulates through cooling towers or over wet pads that are exposed to air, and the portion that evaporates carries heat away with it. Large amounts of water can be lost through that evaporation on the hottest days.

In between these two extremes, hybrid methods are common as companies try to find a balance.

Some data centers, like Equinix's in San Jose, use dry cooling in the colder months and evaporative cooling when it's hot outside. In Indiana, Meta relies on dry cooling and keeps power consumption down in hotter months by spraying a mist to cool the air instead of relying solely on refrigeration units.

Near some data center projects, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft and others are building water treatment plants to help treat and recycle the necessary water. Meta, for example, spent $100 million on a new water and wastewater system near its data center in Kuna, Idaho.

Outside Memphis, Tennessee, Elon Musk pledged $80 million to build a water recycling plant that's meant to turn treated municipal wastewater into cooling water for SpaceX's Colossus data centers. The first data center there, Colossus 1, came online rapidly in 2024, but construction on the water treatment plant was put on hold in April of this year.

Colossus 1 has a water allocation of up to 1.28 million gallons per day, although it hasn't yet reached that threshold, according to the local utility, Memphis Light, Gas and Water.

Tech firms are also building their own power plants on some data center campuses in an effort to secure enough supply, but those also consume a large amount of water. Colossus gets energy from gas-powered turbines it installed on-site, which have been the subject of multiple lawsuits over alleged Clean Air Act violations and noise pollution.

Meanwhile, some chip companies are directly working on ways to bring the heat of a chip down so less cooling will be needed to begin with.

South Korean startup DeepX makes an AI processor it says runs up to 100°F cooler than competitors. Hyundai Motor will use the DeepX chip in robotics offerings.

SK Hynix, the world's leading maker of high-bandwidth memory, told CNBC in July that it's working on a low-heat memory technology called iHBM that can reduce thermal resistance by 30%.

At Equinix, Wellise said each new site undergoes a water risk analysis to determine which external cooling method to deploy in areas with scarce water.

"Communities are beginning to ask the right questions. They're concerned about water consumption," Wellise said. "We take resource consumption, resource use very seriously."

#2902. Chinese AI lab Moonshot turned to Europe for Nvidia Blackwell access amid U.S. China curbs, sources tell CNBC

Moonshot AI, a Chinese artificial intelligence lab that the White House accused of accessing Nvidia advanced chips remotely in East Asia, has sought access to the chips through data centers in Europe, sources have told CNBC.

Deals have been discussed this year for Moonshot to rent capacity from neoclouds hosting Nvidia graphics processing units, or GPUs, at facilities in Europe, four people familiar with the matter said. They asked not to be named because they were discussing private information.

Nvidia's effective monopoly over the most powerful chips has made U.S. export controls a key tool in Washington's effort to preserve its advantage over China in the AI race. American AI labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic are locked in a training and pricing battle with Chinese developers, including Moonshot AI, which makes the Kimi family of models.

Moonshot was looking to lease capacity powered by Nvidia's Blackwell systems, advanced chips currently subject to those export controls which effectively ban their import into China, three of the sources told CNBC.

In July, the White House said the Chinese AI lab had accessed Nvidia's Blackwell chips via a facility in Thailand.

U.S. export controls restrict the shipment of Nvidia's most advanced chips to China, but remote access via overseas data centers offers a loophole. Current law does not give regulators broad authority to control companies' access to those chips through cloud services hosted overseas.

Moonshot, Nvidia, the White House, the U.S. Department of Commerce, China's embassy in the U.K. and its Ministry of Foreign Affairs did not respond to a request for comment.

Chinese developers have made notable capability gains in the past six months and greater access to Nvidia chips could put even more pressure on their American rivals.

To lease Nvidia computing power in Europe, a Chinese AI company would typically rent capacity from a non-Chinese neocloud or data center operator that can purchase advanced chips.

Moonshot was interested in leasing capacity on clusters for inference, which refers to serving models to end users, in Europe, two of the sources said. One of the sources said they were also aware of interest in clusters for training.

Talks for arrangements that would see Moonshot rent access to clusters of 8,000 or more Blackwell GPUs hosted in the region have taken place, one source said. They described the Chinese AI lab as "very aggressive" in its pursuit of more computing power.

One of the sources told CNBC that talks on securing Moonshot compute in Europe had taken place since the White House said it had done so via Thailand. Moonshot did not comment at the time.

Founded in 2023, Beijing-based Moonshot AI is one of China's leading model builders. It released a new model in July that Moonshot said closed the gap with leading systems built by Anthropic and OpenAI.

The model saw significant uptake as a cheaper alternative to U.S. offerings. Chinese AI models have surged in global adoption in 2026, amid capability gains and lower costs than U.S. rivals.

Around the time Kimi K3 was released, Moonshot said demand had stretched its capacity, and it temporarily paused new subscriptions to prioritize compute for current members. "We're adding capacity as fast as we can and will reopen new subscription spots in batches," the company said in a LinkedIn post.

Leasing AI compute is a booming industry, with customers, or offtakers, typically taking capacity for a fee and paying an upfront cost to secure the deal, which is a percentage of the lifetime contract value.

One source said it had been proposed that Moonshot pay 30% to 50% more upfront than a non-Chinese offtaker would.

Arrangements with Chinese customers can be more lucrative upfront, as neoclouds and financing firms look to hedge against a potential future ban of remote access agreements.

In Washington, lawmakers have proposed the Remote Access Security Act (RASA) to expand U.S. export controls to include the cloud-based access of critical hardware and software. It passed the House of Representatives in January but has not passed the Senate.

The Financial Times reported on Tuesday that the chip giant told cloud providers selling access to their chips that they must sell at least 51% of services to non-Chinese customers.

Asked about the reports earlier this week, an Nvidia spokesperson said: "America cannot win the AI race unless America's AI infrastructure wins worldwide — if the world's clouds rely on foreign-made chips, the model and application layers will be foreign-built as well."

Nvidia said in August that Washington had licensed some sales of its H200 AI chips, an older generation, but that "such sales were restricted" by the Chinese government, which has sought to boost domestic chipmakers by limiting access to Nvidia chips.

#2900. How the midterms could fuel the K-shaped AI trade

The U.S. midterms could widen the AI divide: tighter data center rules may reward tech giants with capacity in hand and squeeze debt-funded companies trying to catch up.

Democratic victories in key gubernatorial races raised the prospect of tougher scrutiny for data center projects, adding to investor concerns about how quickly new capacity can be built and financed.

In Texas, Democratic governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has proposed a statewide moratorium on new data center permits, although projects already under construction would reportedly be exempt.

As the markets digested the results on Wednesday, Microsoft and Amazon each surged more than 6%, while AI infrastructure provider CoreWeave plunged around 10%. Chipmakers Micron and Nvidia fell 7% and 3.17%, respectively.

Differences in borrowing costs are playing a key role in the split, James Sullivan, managing director and co-head of global fundamental research at JPMorgan, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Thursday.

Sullivan said borrowing spreads have widened significantly for neocloud companies, leaving their cost of additional capital substantially higher than that of hyperscalers that benefit from more diversified customers and revenue streams.

"The fact that you're seeing this bifurcation in equity performance is largely driven by the view that existing capacity is significantly more valuable now because of incremental supply constraints," he said.

The midterm results could reinforce that view if they make new projects more vulnerable to permitting delays.

BMI warned that a sharp slowdown in data center construction and AI investment could produce a greater-than-expected decline in overall investment, The Business Times reported, citing the firm's post-midterm analysis.

The potential impact is uneven across the construction pipeline. Much of the data center capacity expected to come online in 2027 is already under construction, limiting the near-term impact of new restrictions.

Projects planned for 2028 face greater exposure to permitting delays and regulatory uncertainty, investment bank Nomura said in a note Wednesday about the midterm results.

Tighter regulations on data center construction could become a more significant headwind to AI investment from 2028, the firm said.

Shares of Nvidia-backed neocloud CoreWeave, Nebius and Iren fell at least 30% in October, as tightening credit markets raised questions about how AI infrastructure projects would be financed. CoreWeave's five-year credit default swap spread has surged past 1,100 basis points, reflecting rising costs for investors seeking protection against a potential default.

The distinction may be how well companies can absorb delays and fund the capacity they do build. Hyperscalers have broader cloud businesses and more internally generated cash flow, while lower-rated neoclouds face greater exposure to borrowing costs, energy prices and refinancing conditions, JPMorgan Asset Management said in an Oct. 20 research note.

The asset manager said financially stronger hyperscalers could also secure cheaper capacity or acquire assets if weaker operators scale back.
